🌐 Academic trial presentation + registryNovember 9–10, 2026
An LNP-delivered in vivo edit needs no autologous slot — but this readout is 30-day biochemistry, not a manufacturing change

A Cleveland Clinic Phase 1b trial of CTX310, CRISPR Therapeutics’ in vivo CRISPR-Cas9 base editing therapy targeting the ANGPTL3 gene, produced a large triglyceride reduction after a single intravenous infusion, presented in a late-breaking science session at the American Heart Association’s annual meeting in Chicago on November 9, 2026. Results as reported: in 10 adults with severe hypertriglyceridemia, 30 days after a single infusion, triglycerides fell an average of 63%, with reductions up to 85%, and ANGPTL3 levels dropped by more than 65%; non-HDL cholesterol, apolipoprotein B and triglyceride-rich lipoprotein cholesterol were also lowered. The company's own release for the same presentation, which was not cited when this item was first published, quantifies what the Cleveland Clinic release left qualitative: mean reductions of 51% in non-HDL-C and 23% in apolipoprotein B (ApoB), and a mean LDL-C reduction of 16% from a low mean baseline of 60 mg/dL. It also fixes the design: a fixed flat dose of 40 mg for patients under 85 kg and 50 mg at 85 kg or above, designed to approximate the 0.8 mg per kg dose used in the Phase 1a trial; at baseline the cohort had a median triglyceride level above 1,000 mg/dL, all participants had diabetes, three (30%) had a history of pancreatitis and two (20%) had clinical atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. On durability, the company reports that reductions in ANGPTL3 and triglycerides were maintained in the seven participants followed for at least 90 days — the longest follow-up disclosed for this cohort, and a 90-day signal rather than a one-year one. On safety, the company reports no serious adverse events related to CTX310 and no clinically relevant AST or ALT elevations; one participant had a Grade 2 infusion-related reaction, which resolved and which that participant completed the infusion through; and there were no Grade 3 or higher adverse events related to CTX310. No events of acute pancreatitis were reported on study, in a population where 30% of participants had a history of pancreatitis — the more informative safety statement for this indication, and one the Cleveland Clinic release did not make. The Cleveland Clinic release separately reported that the treatment was generally well tolerated, with no serious side effects related to the treatment observed. The mechanism is in vivo editing: the therapy switches off a gene that contributes to elevated triglyceride levels, which is why a one-time treatment is possible. What this is not: a durability result. The Cleveland Clinic release states the hope is that the approach could provide long-lasting benefits without ongoing medication, and that additional research is needed to determine how durable the effects may be. Thirty-day biochemical response is not proof of a one-time cure in a chronic metabolic disease, and the release says so. The study builds on earlier Phase 1 research across lipid disorders and multiple doses, and was funded by CRISPR Therapeutics AG. Registry (v2 API; robots.txt read first this session): NCT07491172 (CRSP-CVD-400) is a recruiting Phase 1 open-label, multicenter, first-in-human ascending-dose trial of CTX310 in refractory dyslipidemias, 90 estimated participants, sponsor CRISPR Therapeutics AG, actual start June 21, 2024, estimated primary completion June 2027, last update posted September 18, 2026, 17 sites across Australia, New Zealand, the United Kingdom and the United States. Why this is a separate item rather than an update to the site’s November 2 coverage: that item reported the RMAT designations and one-year durability data from the company’s earlier presentation; this is the first disclosure of the Cleveland Clinic Phase 1b dataset, and it is a different readout from a different study. Scope boundary carried in the item: the Cleveland Clinic release is the source of record here, and it concerns CTX310 — a gene-editing therapy — not the Arrowhead ARO-DIMER-PA small interfering RNA study, which this bulletin has held as out of scope in each of the last four cycles.

🌐 Cleveland Clinic — CTX310 Phase 1b results at the AHA Scientific Sessions, November 9, 2026 · 🌐 CRISPR Therapeutics — Phase 1b CTX310 data release, November 9, 2026 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07491172 — CTX310 Phase 1
🌐 Peer-reviewed consensus guidanceNovember 9–10, 2026
Freeze timing and temperature are a specification, not a local preference — and comparability depends on it

The European Bone Marrow Transplantation group’s practice harmonisation and guidelines committee published consensus recommendations in Bone Marrow Transplantation (November 9, 2026) on cryopreservation of academic CAR T-cell products — a methodology paper for centres that manufacture CAR T-cells in-house rather than under a licence. The motivation is stated plainly: academic CAR T-cell manufacturing is expanding rapidly across Europe while cryopreservation practice remains heterogeneous, which raises concerns about product comparability. An EBMT survey of 52 academic centres found wide variability in freezing timing, cryomedium composition, device choice, storage temperature and post-thaw quality control, and no harmonised guidance existed for CAR T-cell products. The recommendations themselves are the usable output: perform cryopreservation as soon as possible after manufacturing, preferably within 48 hours; begin freezing within 30 minutes of cryomedium addition; use controlled-rate freezing to at least −80 °C, with long-term storage below −120 °C wherever feasible; and treat retention samples, post-thaw viability, identity testing and validated transportation procedures as mandatory. Liquid nitrogen and Peltier-based controlled-rate freezing systems were regarded as technically equivalent, and −80 °C passive freezing — the workhorse method in most academic centres — was noted as successfully used and acceptable as a back-up method where local confirmation of suitability is provided through formal method validation. Why it belongs in a manufacturing-facing bulletin: this is the first harmonised reference for how an academic or hospital cell-therapy unit should freeze and store the product it manufactures itself. For centres without a licence agreement and a validated commercial freeze protocol, it supplies the technical specification — timing, temperature, device equivalence and the mandatory post-thaw and transport checks — rather than leaving each site to invent its own. It also makes the comparability problem explicit: if freezing practice varies across sites, differences in product characteristics cannot be attributed to the product alone. Scope: the recommendations are consensus-based technical guidance for groups establishing academic CAR T-cell manufacturing, written by a 12-author expert panel after an EBMT workshop using the society’s harmonisation methodology, drawing on survey data, literature and professional experience. It is a preview of subscription content; the item is bounded to the abstract and figure information. The site had no prior coverage of EBMT guidance or CAR T-cell cryopreservation.

🌐 Bone Marrow Transplantation — EBMT consensus recommendations for cryopreservation of academic CAR T-cell products, November 9, 2026 · 🌐 Nature — article page (BMT)
🌐 Company release + registryNovember 9–10, 2026
An autologous base-edited product: still one manufacturing slot and one conditioning per patient

Beam Therapeutics reported on November 10 that its Phase 1/2 BEACON study of risto-cel (ristoglogene autogetemcel, formerly BEAM-101) achieved its primary efficacy endpoint in sickle cell disease — freedom from severe vaso-occlusive crises for at least 12 consecutive months (VF12) on independently adjudicated results — and met all primary safety endpoints at the prespecified interim analysis. All secondary efficacy endpoints were met, including freedom from inpatient hospitalisation for severe VOCs for 12 consecutive months. As of an October 20, 2026 data cutoff, 39 adults and 10 adolescents had been treated and 31 patients had completed the required follow-up for the VF12 endpoint. Safety findings were consistent with previously reported BEACON data and with the known and intended effects of plerixafor use and the cell collection process, myeloablative conditioning with busulfan, the autologous HSCT process, and the underlying disease. Beam expects to submit a US biologics license application as early as year-end 2026, which the company says would have the potential to be the first BLA for a base editing therapy; detailed pivotal results are planned for a medical meeting in 2027, with an earlier data cut-off to be presented orally at ASH on December 12–15, 2026. Mechanism: risto-cel is a one-time autologous cell therapy of CD34+ hematopoietic stem and progenitor cells base-edited in the promoter regions of the HBG1/2 genes, administered via a hematopoietic stem cell transplant procedure. The edit is designed to stop the transcriptional repressor BCL11A binding the promoter without disrupting BCL11A expression, raising production of non-sickling and anti-sickling fetal hemoglobin — the same therapeutic logic as Casgevy, but by base editing rather than CRISPR nuclease disruption of BCL11A. Registry (v2 API; robots.txt read first this session): the BEACON study is NCT05456880 — active not recruiting Phase 1/2 of a single dose of autologous CD34+ base-edited HSPCs in sickle cell disease and severe vaso-occlusive crises, 15 participants, sponsor Beam Therapeutics, actual start 2022-08-30, estimated primary completion 2028-02, last update posted 2025-12-16, 19 US sites. The long-term follow-up is NCT07373639 — recruiting, 50 estimated, actual start 2025-12-15, estimated primary completion 2043-02-12, last update posted 2026-01-28, 17 US sites. Discrepancy recorded in the item rather than smoothed over: the release describes 49 patients treated and 31 completing VF12 follow-up, while the registry record still shows 15 participants and was last updated in December 2025 — the registry has not caught up with the trial. Boundaries carried: an interim analysis of an open-label, single-arm trial, with the full dataset due in 2027; the company itself lists as a risk factor whether topline results from a prespecified interim analysis will be consistent with final or more detailed results, and whether the results will be sufficient to support a BLA. The VF12 figure is on the 31 patients with complete follow-up, not all 49 treated.

🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 9, 2026
An off-the-shelf NK cell product with no apheresis, no per-patient slot and no lymphodepletion-related toxicity signal

Nkarta presented initial clinical data for NKX019, its allogeneic, off-the-shelf CD19-directed CAR natural killer cell therapy, in idiopathic inflammatory myopathy and systemic sclerosis at ACR Convergence 2026 — the first human data from a CAR NK-cell therapy in autoimmune disease. All data are as of a September 18, 2026 cut-off. Patients at the 4 billion-cell dose level received three doses of NKX019 on Days 0, 3 and 7 following lymphodepleting preconditioning, for a total of 12 billion cells. In myositis: among the six patients at the 4 billion-cell dose with at least three months of follow-up, five (83%) achieved moderate or major Total Improvement Score responses, spanning three disease subtypes — dermatomyositis, juvenile dermatomyositis and antisynthetase syndrome; five of six (83%) also improved on Manual Muscle Testing 8, and four of eight evaluable (50%) achieved clinically relevant reductions in skin disease activity on the Cutaneous Dermatomyositis Disease Area and Severity Index. In systemic sclerosis: six of seven (86%) achieved an rCRISS-25 response or greater and five of seven (71%) an rCRISS-50 response or greater, with a median 15-point reduction in modified Rodnan skin score at Day 90 and five of seven (71%) achieving at least a 50% reduction from baseline. The safety finding is the significant one for a cell-therapy audience: across 40 patients treated across five autoimmune indications and three dose levels, there were no cases of cytokine release syndrome of any grade, no ICANS, no IEC-HS, no GvHD and no leukoencephalopathy (the fludarabine-associated neurotoxicity of record), no deaths, no treatment discontinuations due to adverse events, and no increase in adverse events at higher doses. Deep peripheral CD19+ B-cell depletion was observed at the 4 billion-cell dose. Registry (v2 API; robots.txt read first this session): the autoimmune basket is NCT06557265 — recruiting Phase 1/2 of NKX019 in autoimmune disease, 120 estimated, sponsor Nkarta, actual start June 13, 2024, estimated primary completion April 2027, last update posted June 4, 2026, 19 sites across Australia, Puerto Rico and the United States; the immune-mediated disease basket is NCT06733935 — recruiting Phase 1/2, 240 estimated, actual start November 4, 2024, estimated primary completion October 2028, last update posted October 30, 2026, 12 US sites; and the systemic lupus erythematosus study is NCT06518668 — enrolling by invitation, 6 estimated, sponsor Hospital for Special Surgery, last update posted June 22, 2026. The oncology study NCT05020678 is active not recruiting with 150 estimated. The boundaries carried in the item: these are initial, non-randomised, single-arm Phase 1/2 data with a September 18 cut-off, presented at a conference; the myositis and systemic sclerosis figures are six and seven patients respectively at one dose level. The site carried no prior Nkarta or CAR NK autoimmune coverage.

🌐 Peer-reviewed clinical study + registryNovember 9, 2026
Low-dose lymphodepletion and a single 1×10⁸ CAR T-cell dose — a defined autologous workflow per patient, now with a registrational dataset

The registrational data for mivocabtagene autoleucel (KYV-101) in stiff person syndrome were published in JAMA Neurology on November 9, 2026 (Piquet AL, Zekeridou A, Gergis U, Gutman J et al., "Mivocabtagene Autoleucel CD19 Chimeric Antigen Receptor T-Cell Therapy in Stiff Person Syndrome," doi 10.1001/jamaneurol.2026.4155, 22 authors). KYSA-8 was a pivotal, single-arm, open-label, nonrandomized Phase 2 at three US sites, enrolling from September 2024 to May 2025, with participants aged 18 to 75 with antibody-positive stiff person syndrome and inadequate response to immunomodulatory or immunosuppressive therapies. Of 27 enrolled, 26 were treated (median age 56, range 32–73; 16 female), with median follow-up 6.5 months (range 4.4–12.2). Treatment was low-dose lymphodepletion — fludarabine 30 mg/m²/day and cyclophosphamide 300 mg/m²/day for three days — followed by a single infusion of 1 × 10⁸ CAR T cells. The primary endpoint was met. At week 16, the timed 25-foot walk improved from baseline by a mean of −7.69 seconds (SD 10.03; 95% CI −11.74 to −3.64; P < .001), median −4.8 seconds, a 45.6% median improvement; improvement was sustained (median 44.4%) among the 16 of 26 patients who reached the 24-week timepoint. All four secondary endpoints improved significantly at week 16: modified Rankin Scale mean −0.8 (95% CI −1.1 to −0.4), Distribution of Stiffness Index mean −1.5 (−2.2 to −0.8), Heightened Sensitivity Scale mean −3.2 (−4.0 to −2.3), Hauser Ambulation Index mean −1.6 (−2.1 to −1.2), all P < .001. The safety profile is the part to carry for a CDMO reader. The most common grade 3/4 treatment-related adverse event was neutropenia in 5 of 26 patients (19.2%). Low-grade cytokine release syndrome occurred in 24 of 26 (92.3%; 10 grade 1, 14 grade 2) and ICANS in 3 of 26 (11.5%, all grade 1), all resolving without sequelae. B-cell depletion and clear changes suggestive of immune reset were observed, and at last follow-up all patients remained free of immunomodulatory therapies for stiff person syndrome. The authors describe immunotherapy-free remissions with a consistent, well-tolerated safety profile and potential for durable remission; long-term follow-up is ongoing. Registry: NCT06588491 (KYSA-8) — completed Phase 2, 27 participants, sponsor Kyverna Therapeutics, actual start September 25, 2024, estimated primary completion June 25, 2026, last update posted July 24, 2026, three US sites; intervention recorded as standard lymphodepletion with KYV-101. The registry carries no posted results. The boundaries carried in the item: a nonrandomized single-arm trial with 26 treated patients and a median follow-up of 6.5 months, where the durability claim rests on the 16 patients who reached 24 weeks and on ongoing long-term follow-up. The site carried mivocabtagene previously only as an RMAT-designated programme and a named-patient case; this is the registrational publication.

🌐 Company release + registryNovember 8, 2026
Weight-based dosing and a nine-day manufacturing process: the specification behind a 71-patient safety dataset

Cabaletta Bio presented new Phase 1/2 data on resecabtagene autoleucel (rese-cel), its autologous CD19 CAR T-cell therapy for autoimmune disease, at ACR Convergence 2026, in one oral and two poster presentations. The headline is durability without immunosuppression: 100% (6 of 6) of dermatomyositis patients maintained immunomodulator-free, moderate-to-major Total Improvement Score responses through one year, with 83% (5/6) reaching that level as early as Week 16 — the timepoint corresponding to the primary endpoint of the ongoing registrational dermatomyositis and antisynthetase syndrome cohort — and most were also steroid-free at Week 52. In lupus nephritis, 100% (5 of 5) of patients who had an optional repeat kidney biopsy achieved DORIS remission off all immunomodulators on no or low-dose steroids, with 100% (5/5) showing histologic response or remission. In systemic sclerosis, 71% (5/7) achieved immunomodulator-free rCRISS-50 and 86% (6/7) rCRISS-25 at one year, with 9% improvement in mean percent-predicted forced vital capacity among the five patients with interstitial lung disease, plus improvement on the modified Rodnan Skin Score. The safety dataset is the notable part, because it is unusually large for a cell therapy in autoimmune disease: across 71 patients spanning myositis, systemic sclerosis, lupus and myasthenia gravis, 94% (67 of 71) had no or Grade 1 CRS, Grade 2 CRS occurred in 6% (4 of 71), 96% (68 of 71) had no ICANS, and there were no IEC-HS events. Cabaletta attributes the profile to the construct and production design — a fully human CD19 binder, weight-based dosing and a standard nine-day manufacturing process. Registry (v2 API; robots.txt read first this session): the systemic lupus erythematosus study is NCT06121297, recruiting Phase 1/2, 28 estimated, actual start February 16, 2024, estimated primary completion December 2029, last update posted October 6, 2026, 23 sites across Canada, Spain and the United States; the idiopathic inflammatory myopathy study is NCT06154252, recruiting Phase 2/3, 74 estimated, 38 sites in Germany, Italy, the United Kingdom and the United States; the systemic sclerosis study is NCT06328777, recruiting Phase 2/3, 37 estimated, 10 US sites; the myasthenia gravis study is NCT06359041, recruiting Phase 1/2, 24 estimated, 17 US sites; and RESET-MS (NCT07006805) is withdrawn. The boundaries carried in the item: these are open-label, single-arm Phase 1/2 cohorts with a September 4, 2026 data cut-off and no comparator arm, the biopsy results are from five patients who chose to undergo an optional repeat biopsy, and the presentations are conference data not yet a peer-reviewed paper. The site covered rese-cel before only as a commercial supply deal; this is its clinical data.

🌐 Company release + registryNovember 8, 2026
Off-the-shelf means no autologous slot — but this dataset still needs lymphodepletion to work

CRISPR Therapeutics updated on zugocabtagene geleucel (zugo-cel, formerly CTX112), its allogeneic CRISPR/Cas9-edited CD19 CAR T-cell therapy, in refractory rheumatologic autoimmune disease, with systemic sclerosis data presented at ACR Convergence 2026. In systemic sclerosis, 83% (5 of 6) of patients who received standard lymphodepletion achieved an rCRISS-25 response at Month 3 — including two who reached the more stringent rCRISS-50 — and all five responders have maintained their response, four of them beyond six months. The seventh patient, who received reduced lymphodepletion, showed improvement in multiple rCRISS components at Month 3 but did not meet response criteria. Across the whole basket, as of October 15, 2026, 21 patients have been dosed — four with SLE, eight with systemic sclerosis and nine with idiopathic inflammatory myopathy. All nine IIM patients, including two with immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, two with dermatomyositis and five with antisynthetase syndrome, achieved a moderate or major Total Improvement Score response; two of three evaluable SLE patients have maintained drug-free DORIS remission through at least Month 9, one of them beyond a year. Safety is the part that makes this the strongest off-the-shelf autoimmune CAR T-cell dataset to date. Zugo-cel was well tolerated in all 21 patients, with no dose-limiting toxicities, no CRS above Grade 2, no ICANS, no IEC-HS, no severe infections, no clinically significant hypogammaglobulinaemia, no prolonged cytopenias, no GvHD and no deaths reported to date. Dose escalation continues, with the six patients dosed at the 200 million CAR+ cell level showing higher expansion than at 100 million. Registry (v2 API; robots.txt read first this session): the rheumatology basket is NCT06925542 — recruiting Phase 1, 80 estimated, sponsor CRISPR Therapeutics, actual start March 10, 2025, estimated primary completion December 31, 2031, last update posted October 26, 2026, 17 sites in Germany, Spain and the United States. The hematology basket (ITP and warm AIHA) is NCT07804004, not yet recruiting, 220 estimated, actual start November 1, 2026. The autoimmune neurologic basket is NCT07549698, recruiting, 60 estimated, actual start July 17, 2026, 12 sites. The immuno-oncology study is NCT05643742, recruiting, 120 estimated, seven sites in Australia and the United States. The boundaries carried in the item: the systemic sclerosis figures come from a July 21, 2026 data cut-off at one dose level with six patients meeting response criteria — a single-arm, open-label, company-reported dataset with no comparator, and the deeper cut is only seven patients. The response is also contingent on lymphodepletion: the one patient who received reduced conditioning did not respond, which is a signal in a sample of one. The registry records carry no posted results. The site covered zugo-cel only as a scheduled ACR presentation in the Nov. 2 cycle; this is the data.

🌐 Peer-reviewed preclinical study + registryNovember 7, 2026
A loop CAR is one product with two gates — which changes the assay design and the release specification

A study in Cancer Immunology, Immunotherapy (published November 7, 2026) describes loop-structured bispecific CAR T-cells that recognise CD38 and CD123 simultaneously, aimed at the antigen heterogeneity that limits single-target therapy in acute myeloid leukaemia. The design target is antigen loss: because AML blasts express CD38 and CD123 in overlapping but non-identical subsets, a construct that requires both antigens can still clear cells that have lost one of them. What the work reports: the team generated two reciprocal loop configurations — Loop 123 × 38 CAR and Loop 38 × 123 CAR — and compared them against monovalent CD38 CAR and CD123 CAR controls to separate the effect of the loop structure from the effect of dual recognition. Both loop constructs recognised both antigens and showed significant activity against AML cell lines and primary samples expressing both. In a 12-day mixed-target co-culture, the monovalent products could not recognise the corresponding single-antigen-loss population, which accumulated; both loop products suppressed outgrowth of both reciprocal antigen-loss populations. In cell-line-derived, patient-derived and mixed-antigen-loss xenograft models, loop CAR T-cells reduced leukaemia burden and prolonged survival. The rationale was checked first by reanalyzing two public single-cell RNA-sequencing datasets (GSE241989 and SciLifeLab 23715648), which showed substantial interpatient heterogeneity with partially complementary transcript coverage. The result with the most clinical weight is the toxicity one, and it differs between the two constructs. Because CD38 is expressed on normal haematopoietic progenitors, the authors ran colony-forming assays on human umbilical cord blood CD34+ progenitors. Neither loop construct reduced colony formation at 24 hours, but at 72 hours the Loop 123 × 38 group showed decreased BFU-E and CFU-GM formation while the Loop 38 × 123 cells did not, against vector-T controls — BFU-E and CFU-GM formation was significantly higher in the Loop 38 × 123 group (P < 0.01 and P < 0.0001 respectively). The authors conclude that both configurations retain activity in antigen-heterogeneous models, with Loop 123 × 38 showing the stronger antileukaemic profile but also the greater suppression of normal progenitor colonies. That is the trade a developer in this space has to make explicitly, and it is visible in the data rather than buried. What this is: preclinical work — cell-line and patient-derived assays plus xenograft models, with 16 authors from the group led by Jianxiang Wang. Nothing is in the clinic for this programme. And it is not the first dual-target CAR T-cell to enter the clinic: a trial (NCT07523555, recruiting Phase 1/2, 96 estimated, actual start March 2, 2026, estimated primary completion March 14, 2027, last update posted April 13, 2026), which runs biomarker-selected dual-target modules including CD19/CD22, universal CAR T-cell study (NCT05995041, not yet recruiting, 30 estimated, CLL-1, CD33, CD38 and/or CD123-specific) and the CART123 CD123-directed universal CAR T-cell study (NCT05995041, not yet recruiting, 30 estimated, CLL-1, CD33, CD38 and/or CD123-specific) and the CART123 CD123-directed trial (NCT07464951, recruiting, 30 estimated). So the honest framing is a new design — the loop architecture itself, and the explicit efficacy-versus-haematopoietic-toxicity trade-off between two configurations of it — rather than a new concept in dual-targeting.

🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 5–7, 2026
Community-site outpatient CAR T changes the manufacturing and logistics assumptions, not just the trial

Allogene Therapeutics reported on November 5 that its pivotal Phase 2 ALPHA3 programme of cema-cel in first-line consolidation of large B-cell lymphoma continues to accrue, with the next clinical update anticipated in mid-2027 and trial enrolment expected to complete by year-end 2027. ALPHA3 is described by the company as the first pivotal, randomised trial in LBCL designed to test whether minimal residual disease (MRD)-guided treatment after first-line therapy can delay or prevent relapse, and enrolment momentum is attributed to expanded site participation, increased MRD screening activity, strong investigator engagement and brisk enrolment in Asia-Pacific following a positive interim futility analysis reported earlier this year. The detail that matters for a delivery conversation: the ALPHA3 network now includes community oncology practices where the majority of LBCL patients receive treatment, and early clinical experience has shown the feasibility of outpatient cema-cel administration, including at community practices with limited or no prior CAR T-cell experience. Access preparation is already underway — the company has appointed Rocio Manghani, MPH (from Kite, Gamida Cell and Orca Bio) as Vice President, Market Access, Evidence & Strategy, and is focusing on payer coverage, reimbursement and treatment-site readiness. An investor event at ASH is planned on CARVYKTI and cema-cel adoption barriers. Autoimmune and preclinical: the Phase 1 RESOLUTION study of ALLO-329 in autoimmune disease will be presented at ACR Convergence on November 9, expanding a published nine-patient abstract with additional patients and longer follow-up across three dose levels and three lymphodepletion approaches, including responses in systemic sclerosis. Two invited SITC 2026 presentations cover solid-tumour clinical progress, and a preclinical poster covers a next-generation DLL3/CD70 dual CAR T candidate for small cell lung cancer. The company ended the quarter with $388.9 million in cash, cash equivalents and investments. NCT07085104 — ALLO-329, a recruiting Phase 1 of a dual anti-CD19/anti-CD70 allogeneic CAR T product in autoimmune disease, 66 estimated, actual start November 13, 2025, estimated primary completion February 2028, last update posted November 2, 2026, 18 sites. What is a company statement rather than data: the mid-2027 update, the year-end 2027 enrolment completion, the community-site feasibility claims and the access timeline are all the company’s own expectations and framing, with no clinical readout in this release.

🌐 Peer-reviewed pooled clinical analysisNovember 5–7, 2026
Cardiac monitoring was the open question for systemic AAV infusion; the pooled dataset answers it for now

A pooled analysis across four clinical trials of delandistrogene moxeparvovec reports a manageable long-term cardiac safety profile, with no evidence of persistent treatment-related cardiac injury over follow-up of one to five years. The analysis integrates data from 218 patients across studies 101, 102, ENDEAVOR and EMBARK, of whom 216 received active gene transfer therapy, and evaluates adverse events, routine echocardiography, serial high-sensitivity troponin I measurements, electrocardiography and cardiac magnetic resonance imaging. The reported finding is that cardiac complications were generally infrequent, transient and responsive to medical management. The mechanism is why long-term cardiac follow-up was the open question. Delandistrogene moxeparvovec uses an AAVrh74 vector with an MHCK7-driven micro-dystrophin expression cassette that includes an enhancer active in cardiac muscle, specifically to stabilise or slow cardiac disease progression. Because immune responses against vector capsids or expressed transgenes could theoretically provoke myocardial inflammation, and because published longitudinal cardiac data were limited, experts had called for systematic tracking of left ventricular performance and cardiac biomarkers after systemic infusion. Preclinical work in rodents had shown improved cardiac parameters and lower troponin levels, while some AAV studies had noted myocardial inflammatory changes. The trial mix behind the pooled dataset: the open-label Phase 1/2a study 101 (four participants), the randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled crossover study 102 (41 patients), the multi-cohort open-label Phase 1b ENDEAVOR trial (48 patients) and the Phase 3 randomized crossover EMBARK trial (125 participants). All required stable corticosteroid dosing before screening, except a subset of young children aged under a stated threshold. Boundary carried: a pooled retrospective analysis across open-label and randomised studies of different sizes, with no untreated control group of comparable follow-up, and it establishes the absence of persistent injury signal rather than a benefit claim. The paper is published in Cardiology and Therapy (doi 10.1007/s40119-026-00457-5).

🌐 Cardiology and Therapy — Cardiac Safety Outcomes in Delandistrogene Moxeparvovec Clinical Trials, up to 5 years of follow-up
🌐 Regulator safety notice + peer-reviewed case reportsNovember 5–7, 2026
A confirmed vector-integration signal changes the release-testing conversation for every AAV programme

FDA announced on November 6 that it is investigating a serious risk of malignancy linked to Zolgensma, the AAV9-based gene therapy for spinal muscular atrophy, and that it is evaluating whether regulatory action is appropriate. This is the most significant safety development in the AAV field to date, and it is now on FDA’s own site rather than in reporting. What FDA says, in its own terms: FDA has received postmarketing adverse event reports of malignancies in patients treated with Zolgensma, including confirmed reports of product-related insertional oncogenesis involving the GLI1 oncogene — GLI1 being a gene with the potential to cause cancer when overexpressed or abnormally activated. The reported tumour formations include tumours of the central and peripheral nervous systems, spinal and paraspinal regions, and kidney. Three cases involved tumours associated with GLI1; in two of them, molecular analysis of tumour tissue demonstrated Zolgensma AAV vector DNA integration into the host genome, with the promoter region of the onasemnogene abeparvovec construct inserted upstream of or within the GLI1 oncogene, and the mechanism of GLI1 overexpression in the third case remains under investigation. The scope is wider than one product. FDA has determined the risk of neoplasia, including malignancy, applies to both onasemnogene abeparvovec products — Zolgensma (intravenous, for patients under two years of age) and Itvisma (intrathecal, for patients two years and older) — because they contain the same vector and construct. There have been no reported cases of neoplasia following Itvisma administration to date, and FDA says it is closely monitoring similar AAV programmes. What has not changed, and what might. FDA states the overall benefits of Zolgensma and Itvisma continue to outweigh their potential risks for their approved uses, that Novartis as sponsor is conducting long-term follow-up safety studies, and that FDA is expeditiously evaluating the adequacy of the current prescribing information and of long-term follow-up studies to investigate and mitigate the risk. The current US prescribing information carries only a “theoretical risk of tumorigenicity due to integration of AAV vector DNA into the genome” under Warnings and Precautions. FDA directs that patients and clinical trial participants receiving these products should be monitored for tumour formation, and that providers who develop a tumour in a Zolgensma patient, or who wish to report other suspected adverse reactions, should contact Novartis Gene Therapies or FDA MedWatch. Initial approval of Zolgensma was May 24, 2019; Itvisma was approved November 24, 2025. The two peer-reviewed precedents FDA cites are the reason this is not a surprise: a GLI1 integration event in the FBX-101 programme (Vo et al., New England Journal of Medicine 2026;395(15):1555–1558, doi 10.1056/NEJMc2607927, PMID 42874111) and a PLAG1 integration event in the RGX-111 programme (Ahrens-Nicklas et al., NEJM 2026;394(21):2126–2133, doi 10.1056/NEJMoa2601608, PMID 42127296). Why this belongs in the bulletin. The site has tracked AAV throughout this cycle — including the Nature Reviews Drug Discovery brief published yesterday in which FDA’s own reviewers counted eight approved AAV in vivo products against more than 300 INDs. Those eight products wrote the post-approval framework that this investigation now tests. On the same day, the CBER brief was published, the agency opened a malignancy investigation into the oldest and most widely used AAV product on the market, and the vector’s integration risk was confirmed by molecular analysis in two patients’ tumour tissue rather than remaining theoretical.

🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 5–6, 2026
An LNP-delivered in vivo editor aimed at clearing cccDNA — no autologous slot, but a durability claim that has to run for years

Precision BioSciences presented Phase 1 data from ELIMINATE-B showing that its in vivo gene editor PBGENE-HBV achieved complete and durable loss of pgRNA to undetectable in 100% of evaluable patients at the go-forward 0.4 mg/kg dose, with durable ongoing pgRNA loss in all patients who had at least six months of follow-up. The company frames this as indicative of permanent elimination of cccDNA — covalently closed circular DNA, the persistent viral reservoir that current antivirals do not clear — and says the programme is advancing to a global Phase 2 study to evaluate cure rates after a finite course of treatment and cessation of nucleos(t)ide analogues. The supporting details as reported: across all dose levels, 11 evaluable patients achieved pgRNA loss to undetectable and remained undetectable through the data cutoff; a first HBeAg-positive patient dosed on study went from a baseline of 3.7-log copies/mL to undetectable; and liver biopsies showed a reproducible 1-log and 1.5-log reduction in cccDNA at 0.4 mg/kg across multiple patients. Treatment effect was observed in 100% of evaluable patients as measured by HBsAg reduction. Safety was described as well characterised and manageable across 59 administrations in 25 patients, with no dose-limiting toxicities. Data cutoff September 28, 2026; the late-breaking oral presentation is November 8 at AASLD, and the company expects to publish a Phase 1b data set in the first half of 2027. Registry: NCT06680232 is a recruiting Phase 1, open-label, first-in-human dose-escalation and expansion study of PBGENE-HBV, 45 estimated participants, sponsor Precision BioSciences, actual start November 14, 2024, estimated primary completion December 2026, last update posted August 24, 2026, seven sites across France, Hong Kong, Moldova, New Zealand, Romania and the United States. A long-term observational follow-up, NCT07254208, is enrolling by invitation with 45 estimated participants and primary completion January 2040. The boundaries carried in the item: undetectable pgRNA is a viral marker, not a durability or safety demonstration — the follow-up study runs to 2040 precisely because the durability question is open — and the company states it will be presenting, not that it has presented, these results.

🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 5–6, 2026
Excising exons 45–55 means a single AAV carrying two nucleases — a different construct and release profile from a microdystrophin product

The independent Data Safety Monitoring Board for Precision BioSciences’ PBGENE-DMD cleared the first patient treated in the Phase 1/2 FUNCTION-DMD study for Duchenne muscular dystrophy, with no serious adverse events and no adverse events of special interest through week eight, and endorsed proceeding with dosing additional patients in Part 1. The company describes the eight-week window as the period that matters for monitoring the early safety events known to be associated with AAV-based therapies. The mechanism is the part that distinguishes this from the approved microdystrophin products. PBGENE-DMD uses two complementary ARCUS nucleases delivered in a single AAV to excise exons 45–55 of the dystrophin gene, aiming to restore a near full-length functional dystrophin protein native to the body rather than a foreign, truncated microdystrophin — mutations between exons 45 and 55 represent up to 60% of boys with DMD. The company frames this as a different proposition from truncated approaches that have led to limited functional benefit. Registry: NCT07429240 (study ID PBGENE-DMD-01) is a recruiting Phase 1/2a multi-center, open-label study of a single dose of PBGENE-DMD, 18 estimated participants, sponsor Precision BioSciences, actual start April 24, 2026, estimated primary completion November 2029, last update posted October 21, 2026, two US sites. The primary outcome is the incidence, severity and causality of treatment-emergent and serious adverse events. What is not yet verified: this is a safety milestone in one patient — there is no efficacy data, no dystrophin expression result and no functional outcome in the public record, and the registry still lists only two sites with an 18-participant target. The company states it expects safety and efficacy updates from FUNCTION-DMD throughout 2027.

🌐 Precision BioSciences — positive DSMB safety review for PBGENE-DMD, November 5, 2026 (SEC-filed release) · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07429240 — FUNCTION-DMD
🌐 Peer-reviewed regulatory analysis by agency reviewersNovember 5–6, 2026
Eight approved AAV products, 300 INDs: the post-approval obligations are the specification every programme will be measured against

A team of FDA reviewers at the Center for Biologics Evaluation and Research published an analysis of how the agency has actually handled AAV gene therapies, aimed at guiding future submissions and accelerating access. The headline counts are the ones a competitor should hold onto: between 2017 and 2025 FDA approved eight AAV-based in vivo gene therapy products under biologics license applications, and by the end of 2025 the agency had received over 300 new investigational new drug submissions using AAV gene delivery. The article analyses public and FDA data on regulatory timelines, expedited pathways, communication patterns and postmarketing obligations, and sets out lessons learned. Why this belongs in a manufacturing-facing bulletin. A pipeline count of over 300 AAV INDs against eight approved products is the clearest statement yet of the shape of this field: the regulatory science around AAV has been written by eight products, and the post-approval obligations attached to them — long-term follow-up, pharmacovigilance for delayed genotoxicity and integration risk — are what every one of those 300 submissions will eventually be measured against. Where this connects to live news. The article appeared on November 6, one day after FDA’s finalized potency-assurance guidance for cellular and gene therapy products (the five-part strategy this bulletin reported on November 3) and amid active reporting that the agency is examining tumour risk in children treated with Novartis’ Zolgensma, an AAV9 product. The three threads are the same thread: FDA is, at this moment, writing the post-approval framework for a modality whose approved products are now generating safety signals that the 2017–2025 cohort did not contain. What this is: a Biobusiness Brief in Nature Reviews Drug Discovery by four CBER staff members, with no competing interests declared. It is an agency perspective rather than independent analysis, which is worth stating — and the fact that it is authored by the reviewers themselves is also what makes it authoritative about FDA’s own practice.

🌐 Nature Reviews Drug Discovery — Regulatory lessons from FDA review of approved AAV gene therapies, November 6, 2026 (CBER authors)
🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 5, 2026
If this is approved, there is no cell product to manufacture — which is the whole point of the modality for a CDMO

Intellia Therapeutics reported on November 5 that lonvoguran ziclumeran (lonvo-z) has a PDUFA target action date of March 10, 2027 for its in vivo CRISPR therapy for hereditary angioedema, putting the first potential approval of an in vivo CRISPR-based medicine on the calendar. The company describes lonvo-z as a one-time outpatient treatment designed to inactivate the KLKB1 gene to permanently lower kallikrein and bradykinin levels and eliminate attacks and the need for lifelong treatment; if approved it would be the world’s first in vivo CRISPR-based therapy and the only one-time treatment for HAE. Two facts from the same release change the read on the programme. First, FDA accepted the BLA and granted Priority Review in September and, per the company, advised that it is not currently planning to hold an advisory committee to discuss the application — a meaningful contrast with the advisory committee it tentatively signalled for uniQure Huntington therapy three days earlier. Second, the commercial build-out is already underway: Intellia has completed deployment of its field organisation and is deepening engagement with HAE treatment centres and payers for a planned Q1 2027 launch. The second programme is further from the line: nexiguran ziclumeran (nex-z), in vivo CRISPR targeting the liver to inactivate TTR and partnered with Regeneron, completed enrolment in the Phase 3 MAGNITUDE-2 study in ATTRv-PN exceeding its targeted randomisation of 60 patients, advanced enrolment in the MAGNITUDE Phase 3 in ATTR-CM, and began providing investigators with HLA genotyping results for patients enrolled or entering screening in the nex-z trials — a step that matters because the in vivo product depends on the patient’s HLA genotype. Registry: the pivotal HAELO Phase 3, NCT06634420, is active not recruiting with 80 estimated participants, actual start January 15, 2025, estimated primary completion February 10, 2026, last update posted October 8, 2026, 29 sites; the supporting Phase 1/2, NCT05120830, is active not recruiting with 37 estimated, nine sites, last update posted September 25, 2026. MAGNITUDE (ATTR-CM), NCT06128629, is recruiting with 1,200 estimated across 138 sites, and MAGNITUDE-2 (ATTRv-PN), NCT06672237, is active not recruiting with 67 estimated across 14 sites, last update posted November 5, 2026 — the same day as this release. Financial position: cash, cash equivalents and marketable securities of $614.8 million at September 30, 2026, expected to fund operations at least into 2028 and beyond the anticipated launch, plus a non-dilutive senior secured term loan facility with OrbiMed of up to $400 million. Net loss was $127.5 million in the quarter, up from $101.3 million, with R&D of $83.3 million and G&A of $46.1 million. Collaboration revenue fell to $7.2 million from $13.8 million on lower Regeneron revenue. Why it belongs here: this is a programme the bulletin has never covered, and it is the nearest the field has come to an approved in vivo genome-editing product — an LNP-delivered edit that would be administered once, as an outpatient procedure, with no manufacturing slot per patient and no autologous supply chain at all.

🌐 Peer-reviewed preclinical studyNovember 5–6, 2026
A constant cell product with an inducible promoter moves control from manufacturing to radiotherapy scheduling

A Nature Cancer paper published November 5, 2026 describes a way to make a solid-tumour CAR T-cell deliver a cytokine only where and when it is needed. The problem being addressed is the one that has most limited CAR T-cell therapy in solid tumours: poor infiltration and impaired effector function, while radiotherapy is constrained by radioresistance and metastatic spread. The authors combine the spatial precision of ionizing radiation with the biological specificity of CAR T-cells to achieve localized delivery of tumor necrosis factor (TNF). They identified radiation-responsive genes in tumor-infiltrating T cells and engineered synthetic radioinducible promoters to drive TNF expression; human CAR T-cells carrying the construct (riTNF CAR-T cells) secreted high levels of TNF upon irradiation, independently of antigen engagement — which matters, because antigen-independent cytokine release is the mechanism that makes the spatial control work. Results as reported: in human xenograft and murine tumor models, the combination of riTNF CAR-T cells with local irradiation produced greater tumor regression than either treatment alone, or standard CAR T-cells with irradiation. The response was associated with high intratumoral but not systemic TNF levels, increased tumor necrosis and apoptosis, vascular disruption and greater CAR T-cell infiltration. The authors also report a limitation worth stating: maximal efficacy required irradiation of all tumor sites, despite macrophage-associated abscopal responses following single-lesion irradiation. What this is and is not: this is preclinical work in human xenograft and mouse tumor models, with no human administration; the construct uses a synthetic promoter derived from the mouse Phlda3 promoter, and RNA sequencing data are deposited in GEO under GSE304882 and GSE305175. A registry sweep returns no registered study of irradiation-controlled cytokine release, so nothing is in the clinic. Why it is worth carrying for a manufacturing reader: this is a different design philosophy from the in vivo editing programmes — the payload is not edited into the genome but switched on by an external signal, which keeps the manufactured product constant and moves the control to the treatment protocol. That reframes the release-testing question: if the product is a fixed cell line with a inducible promoter, the lot-release assay is measuring a construct and a basal state rather than a per-patient potency, and the tumour-directed activity is delivered by radiotherapy scheduling rather than by product manufacture.

🌐 Nature Cancer — Spatiotemporal control of CAR-T cell TNF release via tumor-targeted irradiation, November 5, 2026
🌐 Company release + registryNovember 5–6, 2026
UX111 in the UK: the access decision is being scheduled alongside the regulatory decision

Ultragenyx announced on November 6 that the UK MHRA accepted the Marketing Authorisation Application for rebisufligene etisparvovec (rebi-vec, also known as UX111) — its AAV9 gene therapy for mucopolysaccharidosis type IIIA, or Sanfilippo syndrome type A — on October 28, 2026, beginning formal scientific review in the United Kingdom. Acceptance means the application is sufficiently complete for the agency to start its review; the release states plainly that there can be no assurance that marketing authorisation will ultimately be granted and that the product remains investigational in the UK. The access pathway is the part worth carrying. The NICE Prioritisation Board has already routed the treatment to the Highly Specialised Technology programme after agreeing it meets all four HST criteria, including innovation for an ultra-rare and debilitating condition, and the company says it has agreed to schedule the NICE evaluation to align with the MAA review, with the ambition of receiving a NICE decision as close as possible to a potential approval — that is the mechanism by which an ultra-rare gene therapy reaches the NHS in England, and it is now aligned to the regulatory timetable rather than running after it. Context from the same period: FAYUVI received standard full FDA approval in September 2026 with a narrower label than the company announcement implied, and Ultragenyx has already begun the European Medicines Agency MAA review — so UX111 now has a US approval, an EMA review and a UK review with NICE scheduled in parallel. Registry: the underlying Phase 1/2/3 gene transfer trial NCT02716246 is recruiting with 36 participants across five sites in Australia, Spain and the United States, actual start April 25, 2016, estimated primary completion March 2029, last update posted September 22, 2026; the long-term follow-up NCT04360265 is enrolling by invitation with 41 participants and estimated primary completion August 2027, last update posted July 17, 2026; and the MPS IIIA Disease Monitoring Program NCT07855562 is not yet recruiting with 55 estimated and estimated primary completion October 2038. What is not verified: no UK authorisation exists yet, no review timeline is stated, and the company frames the NICE alignment as an ambition to receive a decision close to approval rather than a scheduled date.

🌐 Peer-reviewed preclinical studyNovember 4–5, 2026
A double knockout changes the product specification, not the manufacturing process

A Nature paper (published November 4, 2026) reports the first high-throughput in vivo CAR-T double-knockout CRISPR screen, identifying gene pairs whose simultaneous deletion is both effective and synergistic, and nominating NR4A1 with SOCS3 as a lead combination. The mechanism the authors identify is metabolic: whole-transcriptome profiling and in vivo single-cell RNA sequencing show that NR4A1–SOCS3 double knockout specifically upregulates metabolic pathways in CAR-T cells, with significantly higher metabolic fitness and mitochondrial activity. Double-knockout CAR-T cells and T cells showed exceptionally potent antitumour efficacy and long-term tumour control across different tumour models without compromising safety features, and the combination outperformed several benchmark single-gene and dual-gene modifications in both T cells and CAR-T cells. The authors frame the work as a platform for rapid discovery of combination targets — screening in vivo rather than in vitro — and as establishing NR4A1 and SOCS3 as intracellular checkpoints worth engineering. What it is and is not: this is preclinical work. The screening was performed in vivo in mouse tumour models, and the abstract reports no human data. A combinatorial engineering strategy that raises metabolic fitness and mitochondrial activity is mechanistically distinct from the IL-2-signalling and exhaustion-resistance work this bulletin has covered, because it removes two inhibitory checkpoints rather than restoring a missing cytokine signal — which means it would not necessarily require the same combination-therapy or lymphodepletion adjustments. Why it is worth carrying anyway: if the result holds, the manufacturing implication is that a double knockout changes the product specification rather than the manufacturing process — a more potent autologous or off-the-shelf T-cell product built from a stock cell line, not a new purification or release-testing burden. The paper reports no registry-linked trial, and a registry sweep for NR4A1 or SOCS3 in a ClinicalTrials.gov intervention field returns no study, so nothing is in the clinic.

🌐 Nature — In vivo CAR-T double knockout screen maps synergistic antitumour gene pairs, November 4, 2026 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov — comparator: first-in-human in vivo genome editing in Duchenne muscular dystrophy
🌐 Company release + registryNovember 4–5, 2026
A dual-AAV product for retinal disease means specialist subretinal centres, not infusion centres — and FDA just blessed the CMC

VeonGen Therapeutics announced on November 5 that it has reached alignment with FDA on the design and chemistry, manufacturing and controls strategy for the pivotal Phase 3 of VG801, its dual-AAV gene therapy for Stargardt disease and other ABCA4-associated retinal dystrophies. The alignment came at a Regenerative Medicine Advanced Therapy (RMAT) Type B meeting, and FDA meeting minutes document agreement on the pivotal study design including the primary endpoint, study duration and control arm, together with CMC alignment on use of the existing clinical batch for the pivotal trial and key aspects of the commercial manufacturing strategy. Clinical progress reported alongside it: the ongoing first-in-human Phase 1/2 study (NCT07002398) has dosed 15 patients across three dose cohorts, including adults and paediatric patients, and has shown consistent, sustained functional improvement through Month 12 across best-corrected visual acuity, the Virtual Reality Visual Test and microperimetry fixation, with a favourable safety profile to date. Among the 13 patients with a measurable baseline BCVA, all improved from baseline to Month 6 or Month 12, with 11 gaining at least 5 ETDRS letters, 7 at least 10 letters and 4 at least 15 letters; the company also reports stable retinal structures. The caveats that belong with those numbers: 13 evaluable patients with a measurable baseline, no control group, an open-label single-arm design, and company-reported results — the FDA has aligned on the Phase 3 design precisely because such an evidence base is not sufficient for approval. Registry: NCT07002398 is a recruiting Phase 1/2 single-arm dose-exploration trial of subretinally injected VG801 in ABCA4 mutation-associated recessively inherited retinal dystrophy, 18 estimated participants, sponsor VeonGen Therapeutics GmbH, actual start December 23, 2024, estimated primary completion December 2027, last update posted September 2, 2026. Note a discrepancy the reader should see: the release describes 15 patients dosed across three cohorts at 15 sites in the United States, Europe and China, while the registry record itself lists 18 estimated participants, one location in China, and a last update posted in September 2026 — before this announcement, so the registry has not caught up. The registry also lists the primary outcome as adverse events and serious adverse events, with preliminary efficacy as secondary. Why it matters beyond the company: a dual-AAV gene therapy with no approved disease-modifying option in Stargardt disease, an FDA-aligned pivotal design, and a virtual-reality functional endpoint being pushed through FDA’s Rare Disease Endpoint Advancement pilot programme is a meaningful test of whether the agency will accept a digital vision endpoint in a pivotal trial. VeonGen also has Phase 1/2 authorisations in Europe and China.

🌐 Conference results + registryNovember 4–5, 2026
A one-time intrathecal AAV that stopped on safety: the manufacturing case is narrower than the clinical one

uniQure presented final safety and biomarker results from the discontinued EPISOD1 trial of AMT-162, an investigational gene therapy for SOD1-associated amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, at the 2026 Northeast ALS Consortium annual meeting, held October 27–30, 2026. This is a discontinued programme whose final data are now public, and the most useful thing in it is the disconnect between the two. EPISOD1 (NCT06100276) was a first-in-human, multicenter, open-label, single ascending-dose Phase 1/2 trial with a planned five-year safety follow-up. Five participants were dosed — three in the low-dose cohort and two in the mid-dose cohort. One Grade 3 serious adverse event of sensory-predominant neuropathy, determined to be a dose-limiting toxicity, occurred in the mid-dose cohort, and multiple Grade 2 sensory adverse events occurred in one low-dose participant. The biomarker signal went the other way: CSF neurofilament light chain fell below baseline in 3 of 5 participants at six to twelve months, and CSF SOD1 protein fell below baseline in 4 of 5 over the same period. The investigators concluded that AMT-162 showed possible early biomarker changes, including reductions in NfL, but that the serious and other adverse events produced an unfavourable benefit-risk ratio, leading to the decision to stop development. Long-term safety follow-up of the five dosed patients continues. Product and regulatory context: AMT-162 is a one-time gene therapy using an AAVrh10 vector to deliver an engineered microRNA designed to knock down SOD1 expression, given by intrathecal injection; it held FDA Orphan Drug and Fast Track designations. SOD1 mutations are thought to cause ALS through a toxic gain-of-function mechanism and account for roughly 2% of ALS cases, or fewer than 500 patients in the US. uniQure disclosed the discontinuation with its first-quarter 2026 results but did not detail the timing of the decision relative to the dose-limiting toxicity, which NeurologyLive notes. Registry: NCT06100276 is an active-not-recruiting Phase 1/2 with 20 estimated participants, sponsor uniQure Biopharma, actual start August 1, 2024, estimated primary completion September 30, 2026, last update posted October 22, 2025. Where this sits: tofersen (Qalsody), an antisense oligonucleotide reducing SOD1 protein synthesis, received FDA accelerated approval for SOD1-ALS in 2023 on the basis of plasma NfL reductions and is given by periodic intrathecal injection rather than as a one-time therapy; among AAV-based approaches, Avrion Therapeutics is developing an AAV9 candidate working with EPFL, still preclinical with a first-in-human trial expected in 2028. Why it belongs in this bulletin: it is the clearest recent case of a gene therapy that produced a genuine target-engagement and biomarker signal and was still stopped on safety, with a five-patient dataset and a benefit-risk judgement that has to travel with the data.

🌐 NeurologyLive — EPISOD1 final results at NEALS, November 4, 2026 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06100276 — EPISOD1
🌐 Company release + registryNovember 4–5, 2026
Subretinal AAV for retinal disease means specialist centres and subretinal surgery, not infusion

Opus Genetics announced on November 5 that it has completed dosing in the higher-dose Cohort 2 of BIRD-1, its Phase 1/2 trial of OPGx-BEST1, an AAV gene therapy for BEST1-related retinal diseases — Best vitelliform macular dystrophy and autosomal recessive bestrophinopathy. Cohort 2 dosed 4.5 × 10⁹ vector genomes per eye, a three-fold increase over Cohort 1, and enrolled 11 participants, exceeding its original target of five. The company expects topline three-month data from Cohort 2 in the first quarter of 2027, with dosing in a potential pivotal Phase 3 anticipated in the second half of 2027. The Cohort 1 results the release relies on were reported in September 2026 from five participants dosed at 1.5 × 10⁹ vg/eye: no serious adverse events, no dose-limiting toxicities and no intraocular inflammation, with all treatment-related adverse events mild or moderate; all five showed clinically meaningful improvement in at least one functional measure — best-corrected visual acuity in 60% (3/5), low-luminance visual acuity in 40% (2/5) and contrast sensitivity in 40% (2/5) — with 75% (3/4) of evaluable participants improving on microperimetry and four of five showing structural improvements. Those are company-reported figures from a five-patient, single-arm cohort. Registry: NCT07185256 is a recruiting Phase 1b/2a open-label dose-exploration basket study of subretinally injected OPGx-BEST1 in autosomal-dominant BEST1 disease and ARB, 10 estimated participants, sponsor Opus Genetics, actual start September 25, 2025, estimated primary completion August 2030, last update posted September 24, 2026, five US sites, with primary outcomes defined as the number of dose-limiting toxicity events at the dose tested and the number and severity of procedure-related adverse events. Why it is worth carrying: this is a subretinal AAV gene therapy for a retinal dystrophy with no approved disease-modifying therapy, and the dose-exploration step has now moved to three times the initial dose in an 11-patient cohort — while the three-month readout, which is what would characterise safety and efficacy at that dose, is still months away. Distinguish it from the separate OPGx-RDH12 programme already covered on this site: that is an IND-cleared asset for RDH12-linked Leber congenital amaurosis (NCT07681778), whereas OPGx-BEST1 is for BEST1 disease.

nula-cel still runs the full ex vivo autologous chain — the edit is ex vivo, not in vivo

Kamau Therapeutics reported the first multi-patient clinical data from nula-cel, an autologous stem cell therapy that aims to correct the sickle cell mutation directly rather than compensate for it. The company frames this as the first patients in whom correction has been shown. The release covers three patients treated with the revised V2.0 manufacturing process, two with more than six months of follow-up, plus continued shown." The release covers three patients treated with the revised V2.0 manufacturing process, two with more than six months of follow-up, plus continued follow-up of the first patient ever treated with nula-cel under the original V1.0 process, now four years post-infusion. The data were accepted for an oral presentation at the 68th ASH annual meeting on December 14, 2026, so the figures are from a conference abstract rather than a peer-reviewed paper. Results as reported: all three V2.0-treated patients achieved transfusion independence before hospital discharge, and no vaso-occlusive events have occurred in any patient following infusion. All three achieved neutrophil engraftment at a median of 21 days (range 19–32). In the two patients with more than six months of follow-up, total hemoglobin exceeded 10 g/dL (10.2 and 11.1 g/dL), with low sickle hemoglobin (HbS 17.6% at 12 months and 19.1% at 8 months) and high adult hemoglobin (HbA 76.8% at 12 months and 66.8% at 8 months). Bone marrow HDR allele frequencies stabilised at approximately 20%, and single-cell analysis showed more than 90% of circulating red blood cells contained protective levels of anti-sickling hemoglobin. On safety, no serious adverse events related to nula-cel or the editing procedure were reported in V2.0-treated patients; adverse events were consistent with myeloablative busulfan conditioning and autologous transplantation, and off-target editing remained low. The manufacturing result matters as much as the clinical one: drug product HDR allele frequencies exceeded the 20% target in all three patients (range 32%–38%), and mobilisation required a median of one apheresis cycle (range 1–3) to reach target cell dose. Registry: NCT04819841 is a recruiting Phase 1/2 of nula-cel in autologous CD34+ cells converting HbS to HbA, 15 estimated participants, sponsor Kamau Therapeutics, actual start November 15, 2021, estimated primary completion December 31, 2026, last update posted July 2, 2026, six sites. The registry carries no posted results. The caveats: three patients treated under one manufacturing version, two with more than six months of follow-up, a single-arm trial, conference-abstract data, and a company statement that longer follow-up will be essential. nula-cel holds FDA Fast Track and Orphan Drug designations. Why the distinction from Casgevy matters: Casgevy and other approved therapies knock down BCL11A to induce fetal hemoglobin; nula-cel uses ex vivo homology-directed repair to convert the native HBB mutation back to the normal adult beta-globin sequence. A registry sweep for homology-directed or HBB gene-correction trials in sickle cell disease returned no other registered trial, so this is a new modality in this disease rather than a new target — but it is early data, not an approval.

In vivo CAR T still needs its own lentiviral supply chain — Umoja built one in Louisville rather than outsourcing

Umoja Biopharma announced on November 4 that the first clinical data from UB-VV400 will be presented orally at the 68th ASH annual meeting (December 12–15, 2026, New Orleans; publication number 707, Sunday December 13, 5:30–5:45 p.m. CT), from an investigator-initiated Phase 1 study of UB-VV400 with or without rapamycin in relapsed or refractory B-cell malignancies. This is the first CD22-directed in vivo CAR T-cell therapy to report clinical data, after FDA cleared the IND in July 2026. The mechanism is worth stating because it is the differentiator: UB-VV400 delivers a fully human anti-CD22 CAR together with a rapamycin-activated cytokine receptor (RACR), so that CAR T cells expand preferentially when the patient takes rapamycin — a small-molecule switch over in vivo CAR T expansion. The peer-reviewed description of the platform is a Molecular Therapy paper (published October 7, 2026, doi 10.1016/j.ymthe.2026.08.003, PMID 42576385) showing in vitro and in vivo generation of anti-CD22 CAR T cells and, with RACR engagement, selective expansion and enrichment leading to complete tumor clearance and B-cell depletion in preclinical models; all authors are Umoja employees and equity holders, and the work is subject to pending patent applications. Registry: NCT07735533 is a recruiting Phase 1/2 of UB-VV400 with rapamycin in relapsed or refractory B-cell malignancies, 274 estimated participants, sponsor Umoja Biopharma, last update posted October 9, 2026. 🌐 Company announcement + registryNovember 2–4, 2026
Logic-gated CAR T cells need HLA-LOH prescreening and a paired companion diagnostic — a different manufacturing and logistics chain

A2 Biotherapeutics reported on November 3 that its logic-gated CAR T-cell programmes produced a systemic complete response in a patient with non-small cell lung cancer, still ongoing more than 12 months after treatment, alongside a complete response in metastatic anal cancer and a partial response in head and neck cancer. The company states these are the first reported complete responses to CAR T-cell therapy in lung cancer and in metastatic anal cancer respectively — claims attributed to the company, and the underlying registry records carry no posted efficacy results. EVEREST-2 (NCT06051695) — autologous, mesothelin-directed: as of September 1, 2026, 16 patients were enrolled across two arms — 13 on A2B694 (an MSLN CAR T activator combined with an HLA-A*02 blocker) across four dose levels, and three on A2B543 (the same Tmod construct plus an NFAT-driven membrane-tethered IL-12 booster) at dose level 1. Tumor types were colorectal (n=4), pancreatic (n=3), ovarian (n=3), NSCLC (n=1), gastroesophageal (n=1) and mesothelioma (n=1). Among 13 evaluable patients in Arm 1, best responses were complete response (n=1), stable disease (n=8) and progressive disease (n=4); the CR was in the NSCLC patient, confirmed at Day 90 by central review per RECIST 1.1 and ongoing past 12 months. In Arm 2, two evaluable pancreatic patients had stable disease. Safety: no dose-limiting toxicities and no grade 4 Tmod-related events across all 16 patients; one grade 1 CRS in Arm 2, and in Arm 1 one grade 3 ICANS, one grade 1 ICANS and one grade 1 CRS. Serum IL-12 stayed in the normal range below 5 pg/mL through Day 56 in both A2B543 patients, and the maximum tolerated dose had not been reached. DENALI-1 (NCT06682793) — allogeneic, EGFR-directed: as of August 21, 2026, 11 patients received A2B395 (anal n=1, head and neck n=1, NSCLC n=2, colorectal n=4, breast n=3; median age 56, 80% women). A patient with unresectable anal cancer after five prior chemotherapy regimens and two courses of radiotherapy achieved a complete response on Day 28 with resolution of all target and non-target lesions and no serious adverse events; a patient with refractory head and neck cancer achieved a partial response on Day 180 with ongoing disease control at six months. Among nine efficacy-evaluable patients, best responses were CR (n=1), PR (n=1), SD (n=5) and PD (n=2) — a disease control rate of 78%. Safety: no DLTs, no GVHD and no IEC-HS; one patient with grade 1 CRS and one with grade 2, both resolved. Caveats that belong with the numbers: both studies are early-phase, open-label and single-arm, the response sets are 9 and 13 evaluable patients, and the abstracts were presented at the SITC annual meeting (November 4–8, 2026) rather than published in a peer-reviewed journal. The registry records are recruiting Phase 1/2 studies with far larger planned enrollment (474 for EVEREST-2, 240 for DENALI-1), all sites in the United States, and both were last updated before this data was presented.

🌐 Peer-reviewed clinical studyNovember 2–4, 2026
Delayed immune recovery after CAR T cells is a manufacturing-relevant signal, not only an oncology one

A study in Nature Genetics (published November 2, 2026, open access) reports that clonal hematopoiesis expanding after CD19 CAR T-cell therapy is linked to prolonged cytopenias, impaired immune recovery and treatment-related myeloid malignancy — a long-running safety question for the modality. The work profiled lymphoma patients receiving CD19-directed CAR T cells (axi-cel) and compared clinical outcomes against propensity-matched autologous haematopoietic cell transplant (autoHCT) controls, 69 patients per arm (LBCL, January 2018 to July 2022). The finding is not that CAR T cells cause more infections than any other therapy — it is that CAR-treated patients fare worse than transplant patients on this specific axis. CAR-treated patients had a significantly higher cumulative incidence of grade 3 or higher infections (HR 2.54; 95% CI 1.26 to 5.41), higher non-relapse mortality beyond day 30 (P = 0.027) driven mainly by infection deaths, and delayed immune reconstitution — mean recovery differences of WBC −2.08, ANC −0.99 and ALC −0.796 × 10⁹ cells per litre per day against controls. The population-level tMN number is the one to carry: screening 533 post-CAR-treated NHL patients found 17 who developed treatment-related myeloid neoplasm, an incidence of 4.2% at three years, with a median time to onset of 741 days. Among patients with at least 90 days of follow-up (n = 458), a TP53 mutation raised the odds of tMN (OR 35.35; 95% CI 3.94 to 4601.07; P = 3.32 × 10⁻⁴) while a DNMT3A mutation was protective (OR 0.085; 95% CI 5.73 × 10⁻⁴ to 0.95; P = 0.045), and receiving multiple cellular therapies before transformation was significantly associated with tMN (OR 3.57; 95% CI 1.33 to 9.59). Post-CAR tMN was molecularly and clinically comparable to post-autoHCT tMN, and single-cell DNA analysis showed mostly independent CH clones while CAR integration into CH-mutant T cells occurred frequently. What the authors themselves caution against: most patients with CH expansion do not develop a secondary neoplasm and most such expansions are probably clinically inert; at this cohort size pre-infusion CH did not predict subsequent tMN; CH was not associated with ICANS or CRS; and it remains unclear whether CH causes prolonged cytopenia or is a consequence of impaired haematopoiesis. The authors suggest monitoring CH mutations — particularly TP53 — in some post-CAR patients with cytopenia, and state that pre-existing CH should not prevent patients from receiving these therapies.

🌐 Nature Genetics — Clonal hematopoiesis and cytopenias following CAR19 therapy, published November 2, 2026 · 🌐 Nature Genetics — Research Briefing on the CAR-T hematotoxicity study, November 3, 2026
🌐 Peer-reviewed systematic review + registryNovember 3, 2026
If the toxicity is the conditioning, then ex vivo cell processing remains the binding constraint for autologous edited products

A systematic review published in Gene Therapy on November 3 examined the long-term genotoxic safety and engraftment durability of CRISPR/Cas9 therapeutics for beta-haemoglobinopathies, reviewing 21 clinical and preclinical studies published between 2014 and 2026 under PRISMA guidelines. The headline efficacy figures are the ones already established: exagamglogene autotemcel (exa-cel) achieved transfusion independence in 91% of beta-thalassemia patients and a vaso-occlusive-crisis-free status in 97% of sickle cell disease patients. The contribution is the safety framing. The review reports that severe adverse events, including febrile neutropenia and stomatitis, are primarily associated with busulfan-based myeloablation rather than with gene editing itself, and that gene-editing safety and conditioning toxicity are interlinked because conditioning is part of the process and edited clones might interact with stress pathways during long-term repopulation. Preclinical benchmarks showed negligible off-target activity in most validated platforms, but rare instances of complex structural rearrangements and intergenic off-target indels highlight the need for in-depth, unbiased long-term genomic surveillance. The authors’ conclusion is that these therapies constitute a treatment option within current clinical frameworks but require continuous surveillance, and they argue for a multilayer safety-risk screening and surveillance approach to pretransplant conditioning to address long-term genotoxic concerns and ensure the safety and scalability of gene-editing therapies for global beta-thalassemia and sickle cell patient care. Read it as a review, not new data: it aggregates 21 existing studies rather than reporting a new trial, and it is by two authors in one field. It is useful precisely because it separates conditioning toxicity from editing risk, which is a distinction commercial and clinical discussion of exa-cel and risto-cel rarely makes explicitly.

🌐 Gene Therapy — genotoxic safety and long-term engraftment dynamics in CRISPR/Cas9 therapies for beta-haemoglobinopathies, November 3, 2026 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05329649 — pediatric exa-cel Phase 3
🌐 SEC filing + registryNovember 3, 2026
Ultragenyx is shrinking around its approved AAV launches — the pattern to watch in gene therapy manufacturing demand

Ultragenyx announced on November 3 a restructuring that terminates its Wilson disease gene therapy programme and cuts roughly 20% of its workforce. The company stated that additional data from the dose-finding stage of the Cyprus2+ study of UX701 (rivunatpagene miziparvovec), an AAV9 gene therapy for Wilson disease, including the fourth dosing cohort, showed clinical activity in some patients but did not meet the criteria that the majority of patients achieve biochemical response and elimination of standard of care, which the company required to advance the programme to the pivotal Phase 3 stage. UX701 showed a favourable safety profile across all cohorts dosed, completed study data is expected at an upcoming congress, and the company says it is working with clinical sites and patient advocacy groups to support the community through the transition. Registry: NCT04884815 is the operationally seamless Phase 1/2/3 study of UX701 — safety and dose-finding Phase 1/2 followed by a randomized, open-label, active-controlled Phase 3 in Wilson disease — active not recruiting, 82 estimated participants, sponsor Ultragenyx Pharmaceutical, last update posted September 25, 2026. The broader commercial picture from the same release: third-quarter total revenue of $173 million, with CRYSVITA revenue of $109 million and DOJOLVI revenue of $30 million; two gene therapy BLAs approved by FDA in the third quarter, GENGLYCOS (pariglasgene brecaparvovec, also DTX401) for GSDIa in August and FAYUVI (rebisufligene etisparvovec) for Sanfilippo syndrome type A in September; two priority review vouchers under agreement for a combined sales price of $413 million, the GSDIa PRV already sold for $210 million with 20% of gross proceeds payable to the NIH under a 2018 patent license, and a definitive October agreement to sell the Sanfilippo PRV for $203 million. The company expects to reduce 2027 R&D expenses to under $350 million and 2027 SG&A expenses to under $375 million, completing the wind-down of all ongoing GTX-102 studies for Angelman syndrome, and says it continues to anticipate full-year GAAP profitability in 2027. The read for the sector: this is a commercial-stage gene therapy company cutting a quarter of its workforce and discontinuing a clinical-stage gene therapy while launching two approved AAV products — a reminder that an approved gene therapy portfolio and a failed clinical asset sit on the same P&L.

BEAM-302 is an in vivo edit with no autologous slot — but it is still a per-patient manufacturing event

With up to 18 months of follow-up the company reports durable increases in total and functional AAT, decreased circulating neutrophil elastase activity, and reductions in mutant Z-AAT and toxic circulating Z-polymers, with evidence of AAT production under normal physiological control, and a safety profile consistent with LNP-based therapies. The pivotal path is the news: enrollment and dosing continue in the global pivotal cohort of the ongoing Phase 1/2 trial, which Beam says is designed to support a potential accelerated approval path in the United States, with completion of pivotal-cohort dosing expected in the first half of 2027.
