🌐 FDA approval + registryJuly 1, 2026
Casgevy in the youngest patients — the label moved to age 2, and the manufacturing burden now lands on small children

Vertex's CRISPR therapy CASGEVY was expanded by the FDA to patients aged 2 years and older, with approval letters on March 18, June 15 and July 1, 2026 and a label effective July 7, 2026 covering both sickle cell disease with recurrent vaso-occlusive crises and transfusion-dependent β-thalassemia. The original December 2023 approval was for patients 12 and older, and the approved comparator Lyfgenia remains labelled at 12 and older — this is a product-specific expansion. The CDMO-relevant detail: the label change applies the same ex vivo workflow to a much younger population. Exa-cel is an autologous, myeloablative, lymphodepleting product requiring a dedicated manufacturing slot per patient, a conditioning admission, and a vein-to-vein interval that has to fit inside a child's clinical window. Moving the label from 12-and-older to 2-and-older therefore multiplies the number of small-volume, high-touch autologous batches rather than the number of large ones — and the registry confirms how early that base still is, with the pediatric Phase 3 NCT05329649 carrying only 13 actual participants and the long-term follow-up NCT04208529 still enrolling by invitation. For manufacturing planning, the relevant variable is not the prevalence of pediatric SCD but the rate at which qualified treatment centers can absorb single-patient autologous lots with this conditioning profile.

🌐 FDA — CASGEVY (exagamglogene autotemcel) product page, STN 125787 · 🌐 openFDA — CASGEVY label, effective 2026-07-07 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05329649 (Phase 3 pediatric exa-cel) · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — Casgevy pediatric expansion
🌐 Company release + registryJuly 2, 2026
Thymmune's acquisition by United Therapeutics — what an organ-generating platform does to the manufacturing model

United Therapeutics acquired Thymmune Therapeutics for $140 million in cash plus up to $160 million in earn-out through the end of 2031. The asset is preclinical: THY-100 addresses congenital athymia by converting human iPSCs into thymic cells that, in animal studies, formed an in vivo neo-thymus capable of facilitating T-cell development. Why this is a manufacturing story, not just a corporate one: the therapeutic product is an organ that assembles inside the patient. The cells are manufactured ex vivo and then mature in vivo into tissue that trains the patient's own T cells — which inverts the usual cell therapy workflow and, if it works, would mean manufacturing a cell type whose clinical effect is delivered by an organ the body builds. That is a different manufacturing profile from an infused CAR-T: the payload is a defined iPSC-derived cell, but the value is realised by a regenerative process that continues after dosing. The registry is empty and that is expected — targeted searches return zero Thymmune-sponsored records and zero THY-100 intervention records — so this is a platform acquisition at the preclinical stage, and nothing here yet supports a manufacturing-volume thesis. The strategically interesting adjacency is to United Therapeutics' existing UThymoKidney program: the company is now running two organ-alternatives lines rather than one, which is a different kind of build-out from the single-asset commercial launches dominating recent gene therapy news.

🌐 United Therapeutics IR — Thymmune acquisition release, July 2 2026 · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — United Therapeutics buys Thymmune · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov — Thymmune Therapeutics sponsor search (no records)
🌐 Company release + registryJuly 29, 2026
Vertex and AbCellera on autoimmune T-cell engagers — why a drug company with a cell therapy portfolio is buying antibody discovery

Vertex is paying AbCellera $28 million upfront to develop multispecific T-cell engagers for autoimmune disease, with AbCellera's platform leading discovery and early development and Vertex funding all R&D and holding commercialization rights. The CDMO-relevant framing is a contrast, not a convergence. The strategic logic is shared with the in vivo CAR-T field — programs designed to eradicate defective B cells as an alternative to personalized cell therapies — but the modality is a multispecific antibody, which carries an entirely different manufacturing profile: no autologous harvest, no lymphodepletion, no vein-to-vein slot, and no cold-chain-per-patient constraint, but a demanding expression and aggregation profile that is closer to a monoclonal antibody program than to a cell or viral-vector program. For a CDMO, a platform like AbCellera's is attractive precisely because it externalizes discovery and early development rather than internalizing it. Nothing is registered yet, and that is the honest read: a targeted query for Vertex-sponsored studies with a T-cell engager intervention returns zero records, because this is a discovery-stage collaboration. The measurable strategic signal from the deal is Vertex's own autoimmune conviction — the Alpine Immune Sciences acquisition, whose drug has since succeeded in late-stage testing, and the $10 billion Crinetics purchase into endocrine disease — set against a crowded field in which UCB paid $2.2 billion for Candid Therapeutics in May and Merck, Sanofi and Gilead have all made comparable bets.

🌐 BioSpace — AbCellera/Vertex collaboration release · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — AbCellera/Vertex TCE partnership · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov — Vertex T-cell engager search (no registered study)
🌐 Trade report + registryOctober 14, 2026
Gilead and Vir on stealthy T-cell engagers — the manufacturing question if masking ever reaches the clinic

Gilead Sciences is collaborating with Vir Biotechnology to develop three “dual-masked” T-cell engagers for solid tumors. Vir receives $135 million in upfront and near-term payments and could gain up to $1.74 billion in milestones; independent coverage on October 14 framed the agreement as an up-to-$1.9 billion deal, consistent with contingent value stacked on the near-term payments. CDMO read: if a masked engager ever reaches the clinic, the manufacturing profile is not the cell therapy profile. There is no autologous manufacturing slot, no lymphodepletion, no vein-to-vein interval and no per-patient cold chain — but there is a heavy expression and aggregation problem, and “dual-masked” formats typically mean a molecule more complex than a standard IgG, which is a different CMC risk profile from anything most cell therapy CDMOs carry today. What is genuinely established is limited: Vir sponsors 35 registered studies, all of them COVID-19, influenza or early-antibody-era programs, and a targeted query for dual-masked engagers returns zero records. There is no clinical trial, no clinical data, and the solid tumor targets are undisclosed. This should be logged as a competitive signal from the antibody side of the field — an off-the-shelf route into the same targets where cell therapy is contesting solid tumors — rather than as a program with a manufacturing forecast behind it.

🌐 BioPharma Dive — Gilead/Vir dual-masked TCE collaboration · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov — dual-masked T-cell engager search (no records) · 🌐 Google News — Oct 14 2026 coverage index (Reuters, Fierce Biotech, Pharmaceutical Executive, BioPharm International)
🌐 SEC filing + registryOctober 12, 2026
Aspen's S-1 and going-concern language — what a four-month autologous dopamine-neuron process costs to scale

Aspen Neuroscience filed to list on the Nasdaq, and its own registration statement carries a going-concern warning: on the current operating plan, existing cash and marketable securities will not be enough to keep operating for another year, and the company concluded that “substantial doubt exists about our ability to continue as a going concern”. The prospectus, filed October 9 and amended October 22, still left the offer price blank — the offering had not priced. The manufacturing burden is the commercial crux. Aspen's lead program converts a patient's own cells into dopamine-producing neurons through a process the company describes as taking roughly four months: skin biopsy, cell isolation and expansion in a manufacturing facility, engineering into induced pluripotent stem cells, differentiation into durable precursor cells, then freezing, shipment to the treatment center and surgical implantation into the brain. That is a long-cycle autologous cell product with intracranial surgery at the end of it. The clinical base is correspondingly small: NCT06344026 is a Phase 1/2a dose-escalation study of ANPD001 (sasineprocel) in sporadic Parkinson disease with 9 estimated participants, enrolling by invitation, actual start January 23, 2024 and estimated primary completion March 30, 2027, run across nine US sites with the California Institute for Regenerative Medicine as collaborator. CDMO read: a going-concern warning attached to a four-month autologous process and a 9-participant Phase 1/2a is a demand signal with a very small denominator — it is not a volume commitment, and any capacity plan built on the IPO proceeding would be built on the company's own ability to raise, which the filing itself questions. Aspen recorded a $62 million net loss in 2025 and about $37 million in the first six months of this year, with R&D above 70% of operating expenses.

🌐 SEC EDGAR — Aspen Neuroscience S-1/A, October 22 2026 · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — Aspen Neuroscience IPO and manufacturing · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06344026 (Phase 1/2a ANPD001)
🌐 Trade report + registryOctober 6, 2026
EPKINLY in first-line DLBCL — what a bispecific moving earlier does to the cell therapy volume thesis

Genmab and AbbVie reported that EPKINLY (epcoritamab), a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager, met its primary endpoint in newly diagnosed DLBCL with a 51% relative risk reduction, in 900 patients randomized to R-CHOP plus EPKINLY or placebo. Why a CDMO should care: this is the clearest recent example of an off-the-shelf antibody moving toward the line where cell therapy is most entrenched. The manufacturing consequence is the opposite of a CAR-T launch — no autologous slot, no lymphodepletion, no vein-to-vein interval, no per-patient chain of identity — and it is delivered from a centralized antibody supply. That makes it a volume-capture threat to cell therapy in its strongest indication rather than a competitor for manufacturing capacity. The registry identifies the study precisely: NCT05578976 (Phase 3, 900 participants, Genmab sponsor, actual start February 8, 2023, estimated primary completion June 2027, primary outcome progression-free survival at International Prognostic Index 3–5 over up to approximately 46 months), alongside NCT04628494 in relapsed/refractory DLBCL (483 actual participants) and NCT06508658 (379 actual participants). Constraints that protect the cell therapy position: EPKINLY's current label (BLA761324, effective June 22, 2026) is relapsed/refractory DLBCL after two or more lines under accelerated approval contingent on verification of clinical benefit; the 51% figure is a relative risk reduction on progression-free survival, not overall survival; and immune-related adverse events with inpatient monitoring sometimes recommended remain a real barrier to community first-line adoption. Analyst peak-sales figures of $1.4–$3 billion are forecasts, not guidance.

🌐 BioPharma Dive — EPKINLY first-line DLBCL Phase 3 results · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05578976 (Phase 3, newly diagnosed DLBCL) · 🌐 openFDA — EPKINLY label, BLA761324, effective 2026-06-22
🌐 FDA approval + registryAugust 19, 2026
GENGLYCOS approved: the first gene therapy for GSDIa, and the first to attack the cornstarch regimen rather than the enzyme

Ultragenyx's AAV8 gene therapy GENGLYCOS (DTX401) was approved for GSDIa on August 19, 2026, and the CDMO-relevant detail is not the disease — it is that an accelerated approval obliges the sponsor to supply a defined volume of commercial product for a defined duration. FDA's indication is narrow: reduce daily cornstarch intake as an adjunct to nutritional management in patients eight and older, with continued approval contingent on verification of clinical benefit. The confirmatory commitment is two years of open-label commercial treatment of 50 patients and 20 controls, run through the existing GSDIa Disease Monitoring Program — the registry records that program as NCT06636383 (140 participants, recruiting, 19 sites), which is a long-tail comparator cohort rather than a single launch lot. The pivotal Phase 3 is NCT05139316 (52 participants, completed, 20 sites across nine countries), alongside the completed Phase 1/2 NCT03517085 and follow-up NCT03970278. For a CDMO reading this: a GSDIa population of roughly 1,500–2,500 patients in the US, with an AAV product that has a real, if narrow, commercial lifetime in an ultra-rare indication, is a small-volume, high-complexity AAV program — and it is arriving on top of a wave of rare-disease AAV approvals. The 96-week published data are the commercially interesting part: mean 61% reduction in daily cornstarch intake and complete elimination of nighttime dosing in a third of treated patients is a genuinely differentiated value proposition, and the Rare Pediatric Disease Priority Review Voucher issued on approval was sold for $210 million — a reminder that in this disease space the voucher, not the launch curve, is often the near-term revenue event.

🌐 FDA — GENGLYCOS product page (STN 125858) · 🌐 BioSpace — Ultragenyx approval release · 🌐 BioSpace — 96-week publication release · 🌐 BioSpace — $210M PRV sale · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05139316 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06636383
🌐 FDA approval + registrySeptember 17, 2026
FAYUVI approved for Sanfilippo A — and the label restriction is the commercially decisive detail

Ultragenyx's AAV9 gene therapy FAYUVI (UX111) won standard full approval for Sanfilippo syndrome type A on September 17, 2026 — the first approved treatment for the disease, and Ultragenyx's second gene therapy approval. The commercially decisive detail is in the label, and it is easy to miss: FDA approved FAYUVI for the “treatment of neurologic manifestations of MPS IIIA… in pediatric patients with preserved neurodevelopmental function”. That is a label-population restriction, not a marketing nicety — it defines the eligible population as the earlier-stage child, and every patient who has already lost neurodevelopment is outside it. For anyone sizing the commercial opportunity, the company's figure of roughly 3,000–5,000 patients in commercially accessible geographies is a prevalence number, not an eligible-population number, and the difference is where the volume case is won or lost. The clinical base is the registry's NCT02716246 (Phase 2/3, 36 participants, recruiting, five sites across Australia, Spain and the US) plus the Phase 3 follow-up NCT04360265 and the disease monitoring program NCT07855562 (55 participants, not yet recruiting). CDMO read: this is a one-time intravenous AAV9 dose for a pediatric population with a limited treatment window, and the vector was developed at Nationwide Children's more than a decade ago — meaning the manufacturing and analytical work is long-settled, while the commercial supply ramp is not. A Rare Pediatric Disease Priority Review Voucher was issued on approval, and the EMA has validated the Marketing Authorisation Application, so there is a second regulatory jurisdiction to build for. Company guidance of shipments to qualified treatment centers within 30–60 days is a company statement, not a verified date.

🌐 FDA — FAYUVI product page (STN 125845) · 🌐 BioSpace — Ultragenyx FAYUVI approval release · 🌐 BioSpace — EMA MAA validation release · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT02716246 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07855562
🌐 Commercial manufacturing milestoneSeptember 18, 2026
Andelyn manufactures FAYUVI — what a first commercial AAV approval at a CDMO actually signals

The most commercially interesting fact about Ultragenyx's FAYUVI approval is not the approval — it is that the product is being manufactured at a CDMO site rather than in-house. Andelyn Biosciences confirmed it is now producing FAYUVI (rebisufligene etisparvovec-hopf) for commercial supply at Columbus, Ohio, and describes it as the first FDA-approved gene therapy made on its AAV Curator Platform. The CDMO-implication is specific and worth stating precisely, because it is easy to overstate: what has been demonstrated is commercial-site qualification of a third-party AAV product at a site that has now supported an FDA-approved therapy — a reference, a capability statement, and a precedent in a sponsor-selection conversation. It is not evidence of scale. No capacity figure, contract value, batch size, volume commitment or exclusivity has been disclosed, and the product in question serves an ultra-rare pediatric indication whose FDA label restricts it to patients with preserved neurodevelopmental function. For RoslinCT specifically, the competitive read is that the CDMO commercial-AAV field has a newly credentialed entrant, that Andelyn is positioning on configurability and vertical integration (plasmid engineering through commercial release), and that the era of sponsors keeping rare-disease AAV in-house is thinning. A second-order point: with a Rare Pediatric Disease PRV issued on approval, an EMA MAA validated, and shipments to qualified treatment centers claimed within 30–60 days, the vector demand here is a steady commercial replenishment curve rather than a launch spike — which is precisely the revenue profile most CDMOs prefer and the one hardest to staff for.

🌐 BioSpace — Andelyn commercial manufacturing release · 🌐 FDA — FAYUVI product page · 🌐 BioSpace — Ultragenyx FAYUVI approval release
🌐 Company release + registrySeptember 29, 2026
uniQure's AMT-130: the BLA is filed on three-year data, and the four-year data are already a problem for a CDMO's planning horizon

uniQure's AMT-130 file is worth reading as a planning exercise rather than a headline, because the approval case and the durability case have separated. The BLA was submitted September 2, 2026 for accelerated approval, supported by three-year data against a propensity score-matched Enroll-HD external control, with a UK MHRA filing alongside and priority review requested. The registry shows a small, slow program: NCT05243017 (European Phase 1/2, 14 participants, active not recruiting, four sites across Poland and the United Kingdom) and NCT04120493 (US Phase 1/2, 43 participants, active not recruiting, 12 US sites), with estimated primary completion in 2029 for both. Why that matters commercially: this is a surgical, single-administration, non-manufacturing-intensive product — a microRNA payload delivered by MRI-guided convection-enhanced stereotactic injection into the striatum. There is no autologous cell manufacturing, no vector lot, no release-testing campaign, no vein-to-vein slot. A CDMO has essentially nothing to bid on here, and the site of care is a neurosurgical suite rather than a manufacturing site. What is worth watching is the endpoint substitution, because it sets a precedent other accelerated filings will copy. The four-year analysis missed its composite primary endpoint (cUHDRS 44% slowing, non-significant p=0.144) while TFC held at 61%; the company says TFC will anchor the confirmatory study, and a refreshed 36-month cut on 15 high-dose patients gave 80%/67%. If regulators accept a functional-capacity endpoint with an external natural-history control for a neuro-degenerative disease — and the same three ingredients (surrogate endpoint, external control, no concurrent placebo) are exactly what sank RGX-121 in February — then the pattern that will emerge is approval built on small, externally-controlled, long-horizon datasets. That is a regulatory-environment signal far more consequential than this one program's outcome, and it applies to the many programs with no plausible comparator arm.

🌐 BioSpace — uniQure BLA/MAA submission release · 🌐 BioSpace — uniQure 48-month data release · 🌐 BioSpace — 4-year data market reaction · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05243017 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT04120493
🌐 Company releaseSeptember 14, 2026
Cellectis exits cell therapy for in vivo editing — another ex vivo program retired in favor of a vector business

Cellectis has done something that cuts against the usual read of a cell-therapy pivot: rather than adding an in vivo program alongside its cell therapy, it exited lasme-cel and eti-cel development outright, abandoning an allogeneic CAR-T platform and the manufacturing and cost model built around it, in favor of two liver-directed lipid-nanoparticle editing programs. That is worth reading carefully for a CDMO, because it is the opposite of the usual ex vivo logic. An in vivo LNP program has no cell product, no leukapheresis, no bridging therapy, no per-patient manufacturing slot, and no autologous product to ship — so in commercial terms it is a bulk-material business, not a per-patient-service business. That cuts both ways for this sector. It removes the ex vivo capacity utilization that a CDMO monetizes, and it replaces a service revenue stream with a vector and LNP supply chain that is capital-intensive, lot-based and concentrated. The .HEAL-101 and .HEAL-201 programs are real in their own right — TALE base editing against APOC3 and TALE epigenetic modulation against PCSK9, both LNP-formulated, with liver-humanized mouse data showing mean on-site base editing around 55% and a mean triglyceride reduction of about 45% (up to 68%) without a significant liver ALT increase for .HEAL-101, and epigenome editing above 90% with a mean plasma PCSK9 reduction above 90% for .HEAL-201 — but all of it is preclinical, and the first human data will not arrive until 2027 at the earliest. The signal to carry forward is directional, not scientific: Cellectis is now the third company in this cycle — with ArsenalBio and TScan — to move decisively out of ex vivo cell therapy toward in vivo delivery. The term sheet this creates for anyone with ex vivo capacity is not that ex vivo is finished; it is that the marginal capital in this sector is now flowing toward architectures that do not need a manufacturing slot, and the CDMO positioning question is how to serve both.

🌐 BioSpace — Cellectis strategic transformation release
🌐 Company release + registryOctober 15, 2026
LB2501: a large-cap's first in vivo CAR-T IND, and what a lentiviral in vivo product actually demands of a manufacturer

Legend's IND clearance for LB2501 is the most consequential single corporate event in this batch for a CDMO, because it puts a lentiviral in vivo CAR-T into a US IND with a large-cap sponsor — and the manufacturing consequences differ sharply from the autologous work that dominates the market. An in vivo lentiviral product is not a per-patient autologous cell service: it is a characterized, releasable, scalable vector delivered to the patient, with the T-cell product generated in the body rather than in a bioreactor. For a CDMO, that inverts most of the operating model — batch scale replaces slot count, release testing replaces in-process cell characterization, and the commercial opportunity is a steady bulk supply curve rather than per-patient campaigns. What makes LB2501 a strong test case is the vector design and the claim structure. It uses an engineered, replication-incompetent lentiviral vector engineered for T-cell selectivity, and it is being administered without lymphodepleting chemotherapy — which removes the lymphodepletion step that autologous programs depend on and which is currently one of the main reasons patients cannot access CAR-T at all. The dual CD19/CD20 targeting is a response to antigen escape, which is a real and documented failure mode of single-target products. The clinical evidence, however, is company-reported and small: 12 patients across two dose levels, with the headline dose level 2 result of 100% ORR (6/6) and 83.3% CR (5/6) at a median 2.2-month follow-up, presented at EHA 2026 rather than in a peer-reviewed paper, and the entire safety claim rests on no DLTs, SAEs, ICANS or deaths in those 12 patients. The registry is the more useful document for planning, because it shows the company is running this in China first: NCT07002112 (Phase 1, 30 participants, recruiting, six Chinese sites) and NCT07763938 (Phase 1, 80 participants, not yet recruiting, estimated start November 1, 2026), with the same platform also in myeloma via NCT07399444 (LVIVO-TaVec400, 38 participants) and a withdrawn autoimmune record NCT07583030. Note there is no posted US record for LB2501 yet. For a Western CDMO the read is that the first serious Western in vivo lentiviral IND will be manufactured somewhere, that T-cell-selectivity and transduction specificity will be the release-critical quality attributes rather than viability and expansion, and that the first mover in this space sets the analytical standards everyone else inherits.

🌐 BioSpace — Legend LB2501 IND clearance release · 🌐 BioSpace — LB2501 Phase 1 proof-of-concept release · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07763938 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07002112
🌐 Company release + registrySeptember 15, 2026
Tempest licenses Senlang's CD7-targeted lentiviral vector — a Chinese-origin platform reaching a US in vivo deal

This is a small option agreement on its face, and it is worth noticing for a CDMO anyway, because it is an instance of a pattern that is only now becoming visible: Chinese-origin lentiviral in vivo CAR-T platforms are being optioned by Western companies that lack the vector. Senlang's platform is CD7-targeted, using targeted lentiviral vectors to transduce endogenous CD7-positive T cells and natural killer cells so that CAR-T and CAR-NK are generated inside the patient. That mechanism has a specific manufacturing consequence worth spelling out. Because CD7 is expressed on T cells and NK cells, and because a lentiviral vector is being used rather than an LNP, the delivered product is a characterized lot of replication-incompetent lentiviral vector with transduction specificity as a release-critical attribute — a genuinely different quality framework from autologous CAR-T, where viability, expansion potential and phenotype are the release tests. It also means the manufacturing business here is bulk vector supply, not per-patient campaign slots, and that the site's value is characterized, releasable, scalable material rather than cell-processing capacity. On the clinical evidence, the honest reading is thin: the company's early-observation claim is in vivo CAR-T generation and expansion at the highest evaluable dose with no Grade 3 or higher CRS and no ICANS as of August 25, 2026, and the registry does confirm Senlang as collaborator on two in vivo studies, NCT07775508 (in vivo CD19/CD20 CAR-T in R/R B-cell lymphoma, Phase 1, 18 participants, recruiting, Chinese PLA General Hospital) and NCT07841002 (CLL-1-targeted in vivo CAR-T in R/R AML, Phase 1, 12 participants, not yet recruiting). A registry sweep did not surface a Senlang-sponsored record matching the BCMA/GPRC5D candidate the release describes, so its registration status is unconfirmed. No financial terms were disclosed, and this is an option, not a completed license. The strategic point: Tempest now has two in vivo delivery architectures it intends to run in parallel — LNP and lentiviral — which for a CDMO means a sponsor hedging across two different supply chains rather than committing to one.

🌐 BioSpace — Tempest/Senlang option release · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07775508 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07841002
🌐 Company release + registryAugust 9, 2026
RXIM002 and the circular-RNA in vivo CAR-T bet — one vector architecture, two very different CDMO service models

RiboX's IND clearance for RXIM002 is a small-company item, but it isolates a genuinely different CDMO service model, and that is why it is worth the space. RXIM002 is a targeted lipid nanoparticle carrying circular RNA that encodes a CD19 CAR, injected subcutaneously to generate CAR-T cells in the body. There is no cell product anywhere in that chain. No leukapheresis, no bridge therapy, no bioreactor, no autologous product to ship, and — as the company states — the subcutaneous formulation and the accelerated dose-titration scheme it attributes to the FDA's review of a full investigator-initiated data package. For a CDMO, the service content moves from process development on a patient-specific cell product to bulk LNP and nucleic-acid manufacturing with release testing on a characterized injectable: fewer, larger, more continuous campaigns, with a different quality framework and a different client. Two points of discipline on the claim itself. First, the registry contradicts any implication of near-term clinical availability: the US Phase 1 dose-escalation study NCT07765966 lists 27 participants, status not yet recruiting, an estimated start of February 1, 2027 and estimated primary completion of June 30, 2028, with no sites posted. The evidence base is Chinese investigator-initiated work, NCT07322718 (early Phase 1, 27 participants, recruiting, Ruijin Hospital, RiboX as collaborator), where the company says some patients have exceeded six months of follow-up — company-reported, with no peer-reviewed readout. Second, the "world's first" framing is the company's own claim and was not independently verified here; what can be confirmed is the registry position of the two studies above. Why it matters beyond the press release: if a subcutaneous in vivo CAR-T is workable in autoimmune disease, the entire delivery proposition shifts from a specialist cell-therapy center with a manufacturing slot to an outpatient injection — and the manufacturing sector that has been built to serve per-patient cell campaigns would have to serve a bulk product instead.

🌐 BioSpace — RiboX IND clearance release · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07765966 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07322718
🌐 Trade report + registrySeptember 1, 2026
ArsenalBio's pivot: 99 cuts, a shift to in vivo, and a question about what happens to ex vivo capacity

ArsenalBio's decision to cut 99 staff — the majority of the company — to become an in vivo CAR-T company is the sharpest single data point yet on what the shift out of autologous cell therapy does to companies, and for a CDMO it cuts in an uncomfortable direction. The ex vivo assets are not simply deprioritized; they are gone from the company's own pipeline page — AB-2100 in renal cell carcinoma, whose topline data were expected in March and never disclosed, and AB-1015, both of which the registry still shows as active but not recruiting rather than terminated, and both of which will need eventual closure. The company is simultaneously looking for strategic alternatives for its assets and technologies, which in practice means buyers for programs with no disclosed data. The financial context is stark: $325 million of Series C raised in September 2024 to advance AB-2100, a 2020 Bristol Myers Squibb partnership with a $70 million upfront and a milestone tranche paid in January 2025, and a company that by September 2025 had already cut half its staff. Why the read is not simply "in vivo is winning": a company that raises $325 million and cannot get AB-2100 to a disclosed readout is a company with a science problem, an execution problem, or both, and the pivot is what remains when those problems run out the clock. Presenting it as evidence of in vivo momentum would credit the architecture for a failure that may have been management's. The real signal for this sector is about capacity, not sentiment. If a company with $325 million and a Big Pharma partner could not make the autologous model work, the question for every CDMO holding autologous capacity is whether it was a bad program or a bad model. The answer so far is mixed: the autologous model has produced approved products, but it has also produced companies that burned hundreds of millions without a readout — and the direction of travel, with Cellectis exiting its CAR-T assets and TScan pausing a Phase 3 for lack of capital in the same window, is away from per-patient manufacturing. That is a utilization risk for ex vivo capacity, and it argues for the strategy RoslinCT is best placed to exploit: serving programs across both architectures — bulk vector and LNP material for in vivo programs, campaign cell manufacture for the autologous programs that still exist.

🌐 BioSpace — ArsenalBio layoffs report · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06245915 (AB-2100) · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07285694 (AB-3028)
🌐 Company release + registrySeptember 2, 2026
TScan pauses a Phase 3 for lack of capital — what the sequencing of that decision says about TCR-T capacity

TScan's announcement contains a detail that matters more than the headline: the company is pausing enrollment in a 310-participant Phase 3 because it cannot raise the capital to complete the trial, and it is choosing to spend what remains on two preclinical in vivo TCR-T assets that will not reach the clinic until roughly the fourth quarter of 2027, with a 75% workforce reduction alongside. For a CDMO, the operational consequences are immediate and specific. A paused Phase 3 is not a paused demand curve — it is a cessation of campaigns. The registry confirms the scale: NCT07702578 carries 310 participants, 26 US sites, a June 18, 2026 start and estimated primary completion in June 2029, and it now shows as active but not recruiting. A program that has enrolled and paused will require a decision about the patients already dosed, the vector and cell lots already manufactured or in inventory, and the clinical supply chain commitments already made. What happens to that demand depends on whether a partner takes the hematologic and autoimmune programs, and on whether the paused study is formally terminated — neither has been disclosed, and both are the things to check next. The strategic reading is genuinely ambiguous, and should be presented as such. The company reports all 13 Cohort C patients currently tracked with complete donor chimerism, including two who had previously relapsed, and has committed to updated data in Q4 2026 and results for all patients treated with the commercial-ready manufacturing process in Q2 2027 — which is a real reason to keep the platform alive, because the commercial-process claim is what would make the asset partnerable. But the direction of capital is away from a running Phase 3 and toward preclinical work, and the wider pattern this cycle — Cellectis exiting cell therapy for in vivo editing, ArsenalBio cutting most of its staff to pivot in vivo — is a set of companies reallocating away from ex vivo. For a CDMO positioning around TCR-T specifically, the honest read is that this is a demand-consolidation signal, not a demand-expansion one: the programs with a capital problem are the ones being handed to partners, and the manufacturing for those programs will follow the partner, not the original sponsor.

🌐 BioSpace — TScan reorganization release · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07702578 (ALLOHA-2)
🌐 Company releaseOctober 16, 2026
Estuary's ADPKD bet: local administration, no registry trial yet, and a modality CDMOs can serve

Estuary Biotherapeutics is a launch-stage company with $103 million and no registered trial, and it is still worth a paragraph in a CDMO bulletin, because of what it signals about where the next service demand comes from. The scientific framing is that ADPKD is a genetic disease whose difficulty is delivery rather than payload — chief executive Wayne Sackett calls local, fit-for-purpose administration the “most crucial remaining barrier to kidney gene therapy,” and the lead programs EST-201 and EST-101 are intended to reach the nephron directly. That reframing has a manufacturing consequence that cuts in favor of this sector's capabilities: a focal, organ-directed delivery strategy implies administration into a defined anatomical target rather than systemic exposure, which in turn implies a vector lot, a defined tissue dose, and a release and quality framework built around a characterized product rather than a patient-specific cell population. It is a familiar CDMO service model — bulk viral vector with tight characterization — applied to a new indication. Two disciplines to hold, because a launch announcement is a financing document. A registry sweep found no ClinicalTrials.gov record for EST-201 or EST-101, so neither lead program has a registered trial, and the company's own site states that EST-201, EST-101 and EST-102 are investigational, have not been approved by the FDA or any other regulatory authority, and that safety and efficacy have not been established. The stated milestone is clinical proof of concept, with no date. The $93 million Series A is real money from investors with domain expertise — the National Kidney Foundation Innovation Fund participating is a meaningful signal of disease-community validation for a kidney-first strategy — but the reader should treat the "world's first" and "paradigm-shifting" language in the release as the company's framing rather than as established fact. For competitive planning: a kidney-directed gene therapy platform funded to the point of proof of concept, with an explicit local-administration thesis, is a plausible future AAV or vector program for CDMOs to serve, and the market being targeted is large enough that a successful first product would not be a niche.

🌐 Estuary Biotherapeutics — launch press release (first-party) · 🌐 BioSpace — Estuary launch release
🌐 Trade report + registryAugust 13, 2026
Sangamo's bankruptcy is a cautionary case: a rolling BLA, a bidder walking away, and assets sold for $161M

Sangamo's bankruptcy is the most useful cautionary tale in this cycle for anyone forecasting gene therapy pipeline value, and the mechanism is instructive rather than depressing. A company with a platform, a clinical asset, a Big Pharma partnership history and a rolling BLA filed still ended up selling the asset for $111 million plus up to $100 million in milestones, and the platforms for $50 million. The failure was not scientific rejection; it was capital. Sangamo's Fabry program is a completed Phase 1/2 — NCT04046224, 36 participants, 18 sites across seven countries, primary completion April 2025 — with a long-term follow-up still active, and a rolling BLA underway. That is a de-risked asset by the standards of the field, and it still went to a distressed sale. The competitive detail sharpens it: Astellas had agreed to buy the Fabry asset after the June strategic evaluation, and then did not, and the trade report is explicit that it is unclear why. For a CDMO, the operational lesson is about how a company's failure propagates into someone else's manufacturing plan. A carrier that lost two-thirds of its value in a Chapter 11 process will have manufactured at contracted rates, and those contracts now sit with a buyer who must re-forecast volume, requalify vendors, and decide whether to continue. The follow-up study NCT05039866 is active but not recruiting, which means clinical supply continues at a reduced rate. Second-order: the two platform sales — Lilly taking zinc finger, capsid delivery and molecular integrase technology plus a preclinical prion program for $50 million — are the tell that platform value in gene editing is being priced as a discounted option rather than as a business. The transferable lesson is about contracting and credit: in a sector where the median outcome for a well-funded platform company is a distressed sale, counterparty selection on long-term supply agreements is a risk-management decision, and a broad sponsor book is worth more than a single deep relationship.

🌐 BioSpace — Sangamo asset sale report · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT04046224 (ST-920) · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05039866 (LTFU)
🌐 Trade feature (in partnership)August 10, 2026
The vector is the drug now: what the ex vivo-to-in vivo manufacturing shift means for capacity and characterization

The single most useful industry document in this cycle is a trade feature published in partnership with AGC Biologics, and it deserves reading in a CDMO strategy meeting, because it makes the operational consequence of the in vivo shift concrete in a way that company announcements do not. Three changes, each of which lands differently on this sector's business model. First, the product under contract changes identity. In ex vivo work the vector is a manufacturing tool and the deliverable is the patient's engineered cells, which means impurities can be washed out downstream and the contract is priced around a per-patient campaign. In vivo, the vector is the drug: it goes straight into a person with no purification opportunity afterward, so endotoxin, mycoplasma, residual DNA and host cell protein are held to injectable-biologic standards and the regulatory flexibility that the engineered-cell framing allowed disappears. Second, the scale requirement moves by orders of magnitude — from 200-litre to 2,000-litre bioreactors, with dedicated procedures for mixing transfection complexes, downstream steps that frequently need re-optimizing because column and membrane formats do not scale linearly, and equipment layout, transfer systems and process flow needing to be planned six months to a year ahead. That is a capital-planning problem, not a scheduling problem, and it is a different conversation from booking a slot. Third, the producer cell line stops being an efficiency question and becomes a regulatory one: the field's reliance on HEK293T carries the SV40 large T antigen, a well-characterized oncoprotein interfering with p53 and RB signaling, which downstream purification mitigates in ex vivo applications but which in vivo is a live safety concern, since even trace residual LTA DNA or protein may represent a safety concern. What this means for positioning: the demand that in vivo creates is large, lumpy, capital-intensive and quality-intensive — a different business from per-patient cell campaigns, and one where a well-characterized, injectable-grade bulk vector is the sellable unit. It is also the reason the workflow and analytical development work in this cycle — from AGC's own interviews to the PM577a and LB2501 items — keeps converging on vector quality rather than cell quality. The honest caveat is that this is a sponsored feature and the framing serves AGC's commercial interests, though the technical content is attributed to named upstream and analytical development managers and the regulatory statements are general rather than promotional.

🌐 BioSpace — feature in partnership with AGC Biologics
🌐 Trial registry (authoritative)July 16, 2025
KLN-1010 — a Western in vivo lentiviral CAR-T in US Phase 1, and what its release criteria look like

KLN-1010 — Kelonia’s in vivo lentiviral BCMA CAR-T — has been in US Phase 1 since July 16, 2025, with eight US sites listed and recruiting (City of Hope, Stanford, UCSF, Moffitt, Emory, MGH, Hackensack, Providence Portland) plus three in Australia, per NCT07075185 (70 estimated participants, primary completion March 2027, last registry update August 28, 2026). Why RoslinCT should care, and why this is published: the point is not the molecule but the correction. An earlier version of this memo carried the claim that one US in vivo lentiviral CAR-T existed and that the swept global count was 20. The registry supports 31 records, four of them outside China, with the earliest Western one — this one — dosing from mid-2025. The CDMO read is unchanged and strengthened: an in vivo lentiviral product is a characterized, releasable lot of vector, not a per-patient autologous cell service. Release-critical attributes for that product are transduction specificity, vector genome titer and residual vector — a different analytical framework from viability, expansion and phenotype on a cell product, and the earliest Western programs are the ones that will set those standards. Kelonia’s platform claims are company statements, not registry-verified outcomes.

🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07075185 (KLN-1010, in vivo lentiviral CAR-T)
🌐 Trial registry (authoritative)September 20, 2024
INT2104 — a large-cap’s in vivo lentiviral CAR-T that has been running since 2024, and what it means for vector supply

INT2104 — Kite’s in vivo lentiviral CD20 CAR-T — has been in Phase 1 since September 20, 2024, with ten participants treated and three listed sites (Westmead in Sydney, Peter MacCallum in Melbourne, Hospital MD Anderson in Madrid), per NCT06539338, now active-not-recruiting. The registry describes the product as a lentiviral vector designed to generate CAR-T and CAR-NK cells inside the body after a single intravenous infusion, with a five-year study duration. Why this is published, and why it matters for RoslinCT: it corrects a claim this memo previously carried — the swept count of in vivo lentiviral CAR-T studies is 31, with four outside China, and the earliest Western example sits inside a top-ten pharma and has been running since 2024 rather than being a 2026 first. The CDMO consequence is the same one that has run through every in vivo item this cycle, but now with a large-cap counterparty attached: the vector is the manufactured product. T-cell selectivity, vector genome titer, replication-competent lentivirus testing and transduction specificity become the release-critical attributes, and the CDMO that establishes those analytical methods early sets the standard every subsequent program inherits. The site footprint — Australia and Spain rather than the United States — also suggests where first-in-human work is currently being run for this modality. Kite’s and Gilead’s strategic rationale is a company position; this registry record carries no efficacy data.

🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06539338 (INT2104, in vivo lentiviral CAR-T)
🌐 Peer-reviewed clinical studyOctober 25, 2026
CAR-T failure has a mechanism — and it moves some of the product specification upstream, into the assay

A Blood paper (MD Anderson with Sanofi co-authors, published online October 23, 2026) identifies a mechanistic driver of CD19 CAR-T failure in relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma: single-cell RNA sequencing of tumors at progression from CART19 found, in a subset of tumors, clonally expanded and proliferating CART19 cells carrying transcriptional features of dysfunction — the signature of sustained tumor engagement and chronic stimulation. Iterative rechallenge of patient-derived CART19 cells reproduced that failure with reduced IL-2 signaling, and the non-alpha IL-2 receptor agonist pegenzileukin (THOR-707 / SAR444245) prevented the dysfunction while improving T-cell expansion and tumor control. The authors then built a cell-line model from a post-CART19 progression tumor that reproduced the originating patient's response and failure kinetics, and the contrast is the sharpest result in the paper: CART19 alone produced a transient response followed by progression and secondary central nervous system relapse, while CART19 plus pegenzileukin led to complete eradication of tumors. Status that must travel with this: this is in vitro and a cell-line model — there are no patient data, and no pegenzileukin-CAR-T combination has read out in people. The CDMO read: if durable CAR-T performance turns out to be partly an IL-2-signaling problem, then expansion potential, memory phenotype and cytokine responsiveness stop being downstream clinical curiosities and become attributes of the product at release — which means potency and functional-release assays for autologous products carry more weight than they have, because they are the only pre-infusion readout of the thing that failed in this paper. There is a second-order commercial consequence too: pegenzileukin is a non-cell biologic, so the proposed combination pairs a manufactured cell product with a second manufactured biologic in a franchise that has until now been a single autologous slot. That does not add autologous slot demand — it adds a partner biologic to the same indication, and it is a reminder that a cell therapy franchise can increasingly be a combination portfolio rather than a single product.

🌐 Blood original study · 🌐 PubMed abstract · 🌐 OncoDaily write-up
🌐 Peer-reviewed clinical studyOctober 25, 2026
Same CAR-T product, very different survival — and what it means for how lymphoma trials are designed

A retrospective single-centre study of 109 consecutive relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma patients treated with CD19-directed CAR-T between January 2019 and May 2025 reports that depth of response and durability can pull apart by disease location. Depth of response was comparable between nodal and extranodal lesions (minus 62.1% versus 65.6% at the first follow-up; minus 77.9% versus 78.1% at the second), yet median progression-free survival was 306.5 days in nodal disease against 90 days in extranodal disease. Extranodal disease was present at baseline in 65 of 109 patients (59.6%) with a higher starting tumor burden (median sum of products of diameters 3573 mm² versus 1998 mm²), and baseline tumor burden was independently associated with both PFS and overall survival (HR 1.17 and 1.21 per doubling). In the exploratory organ-level analysis, pleural, hepatobiliary and adrenal involvement carried the strongest adverse associations, with pleural involvement independently associated with PFS and OS. The authors are careful about the limits — organ subgroups were small (n = 7–19), the analysis is exploratory, and the comparable depth of response at the second follow-up may partly reflect differential attrition, with extranodal patients more likely to be lost to follow-up before that point. The CDMO read: this does not change what a CDMO manufactures — it is one product, one process, one slot. What it changes is who looks like a responder. A program whose pooled response rate looks healthy can be delivering much shorter durability in the extranodal and pleural patients that a real-world referral mix over-represents, and manufacturing providers sit close to that conversation, because site and referral mix is where a cohort's composition gets decided. The practical read is that disease distribution is a design variable for lymphoma CAR-T trials, and that a durability signal which does not show up in a pooled response rate is a signal worth designing around rather than a footnote to the response rate.

🌐 Cancer Immunology, Immunotherapy original study · 🌐 Springer article

The swept count is 31 in vivo lentiviral CAR-T records, four outside China. This review does not establish competitive exclusivity. 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07802717 · 🌐 BioSpace / Business Wire
🌐 Company release + trial registryOctober 25, 2026
First in-body prime editing: PM577a dosed for Wilson disease — and what it means for a CDMO

Prime Medicine dosed the first patient with PM577a, an LNP-delivered prime editor for Wilson disease: the first reported in vivo prime-editing therapy dosed in a person. NCT07748403 — Phase 1/2, 42 participants, recruiting, start September 2026, primary completion November 2028, four sites (New Haven, Chicago, San Antonio, Grafton NZ). It targets the p.H1069Q ATP7B mutation and delivers editing machinery straight into hepatocytes: no leukapheresis, no ex vivo editing, no reinfusion, no autologous product. No safety or efficacy data have been reported; the first patient dosed confirms only that the therapy can be administered, and data are expected in 2027. A second in vivo prime editor runs in parallel (NCT07887347, PM647a, alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, 54 participants, not-yet-recruiting). The CDMO read: this is not a cell therapy product, and for a CDMO it is not currently a revenue event — the trial is a 42-patient study of a one-time infusion with no cell product to manufacture, and nothing about it touches RoslinCT's autologous slot demand. What it does touch is the forward shape of the market. The ex vivo cell therapy model the CDMO business is built on — harvest, ship, manufacture, ship back, reinfuse, with a vein-to-vein slot and a per-patient product — is one of several ways to change a patient's genome, and an in vivo edit is a version of it with no manufacturing step at all. If in vivo editing delivers durable correction in liver, and eventually in blood, the competitive question for CDMOs stops being "how do we win more autologous slots" and becomes "does our autologous slot model still have a market ten years out." That question is worth tracking now even though this trial will not answer it. Two further notes: the registry's primary outcome is safety and tolerability through Week 48, not efficacy, and delivery remains the standing risk for in vivo editing generally.

🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07748403 · 🌐 Gadget Review
🌐 Company release + trial registryOctober 25, 2026
Marengo's invikafusp alfa at ESMO 2026: 8 tumor types, 18% ORR, and a second FDA Fast Track

Marengo presented Phase 2 results for invikafusp alfa (STAR0602) at ESMO 2026: objective responses across eight tumor types among 62 evaluable heavily pretreated TMB-H solid tumor patients, with 56% tumor shrinkage, 18% ORR and 60% DCR. Activity was numerically higher in checkpoint-inhibitor-experienced patients (ORR 20% versus 14%; DCR 68% versus 43%), and responses appeared in both primary- and secondary-resistance tumors (ORR 23% and 28%). In 104 safety patients, CRS was mainly Grades 1 and 2, with no ICANS and no Grade 5 treatment-related adverse events. The FDA granted a second Fast Track, expanding the January 2025 TMB-H colorectal cancer designation to TMB-H solid tumors regardless of tissue of origin. NCT05592626: global Phase 1/2, 366 estimated participants, 19 sites across the US, Canada, France and Spain. The CDMO read: invika is not a cell therapy product and is not a near-term demand event for a CDMO — it involves no harvest, no lymphodepletion, no manufacturing slot, no autologous product and therefore no slot utilization to lose. It matters as a marker. A bispecific that engages T cells without a cell product, validated across eight tumor types and carrying a tissue-agnostic regulatory designation, is the clearest signal yet that the modality — engineered immune engagement — is decoupling from the manufacturing model. For a CDMO whose plan is to sell capacity and execution reliability into cell therapy, the strategic read is that the market it is building capacity for is not the only way the field will be served, and the window to convert autologous capacity into durable, differentiated manufacturing demand is finite. Caveats that belong alongside the numbers: company-reported Phase 2 figures, no randomized comparator, heterogeneous heavily pretreated population, an 18% ORR well below the 56% shrinkage rate, and a Fast Track designation is not an approval.

🔬 Endpoints News🌐 Open WebOctober 23, 2026
BMS patient death in a Phase 1 autoimmune CAR-T trial — the demand-side risk case for autoimmune CDMO forecasts

A patient with highly active relapsing MS died of septic pneumonia 44 days after zola-cel infusion, following unresolved late-onset non-immune ICANS motor symptoms. No further deaths across the 190 Phase 1 patients treated in the autoimmune zola-cel programs. This follows Novartis' rap-cel autoimmune pause after three fatal IEC-HS cases. For RoslinCT this is the most commercially consequential story of the cycle: autoimmune is where the projected CDMO demand curve is steepest, and two of the largest programs now carry a death signal in exactly that indication. The read for a CDMO is not that autoimmune demand disappears — it is that autoimmune programs will demand more rigorous safety CMC, tighter comparability, and faster turnaround for bridging, and that sponsor caution could defer the ramp. This strengthens the case for positioning on execution quality and safety-support services rather than on volume alone.

🌐 Conference coverageOctober 24, 2026
ESMO 2026 solid-tumor cell therapy: a second death, and a large harvest-to-infusion gap

ESMO 2026's solid-tumor cell therapy readouts carry direct CDMO relevance. GPC3 CAR-T in HCC: 16/36 responses, median DOR 4.4 months, related ILD in 6%, and one death from related tumor lysis syndrome and renal dysfunction. GCC CAR-T in CRC: 5/12 responses, 66% grade 3 diarrhea. CD70 CAR-T in renal cancer: 3/9 responses, grade 3 CRS in 4/9. Lifileucel in sarcoma: 10 harvested, only 8 infused. The harvest-to-infusion gap is the operational signal — attrition before infusion is where process development, release testing, and slot scheduling either protect or lose the program. Solid-tumor cell therapy is also where private-equity and venture-backed CDMO demand has been concentrated, so safety-driven attrition in this segment hits capacity plans harder than in the autoimmune or hematologic segments.

Immatics runs an autologous cell therapy and an off-the-shelf bispecific against the same PRAME target

At ESMO 2026, IMA203CD8 (autologous PRAME TCR-T) delivered cORR 55% and median DOR 8.4 months in 20 gynecologic cancer patients at clinically relevant doses, 41% confirmed ORR overall across 90 evaluable patients, no Grade 5 events, MTD not reached. 🌐 Conference coverageOctober 24, 2026
Lifileucel moved to first line in melanoma — a potential volume inflection for TIL manufacturing

The Phase 2 IOV-COM-202 trial at The Royal Marsden, presented at ESMO 2026, produced 29.2% complete responses (7/24) and 62.5% overall responses in ICI-naïve advanced melanoma when lifileucel (TIL) was added to first-line pembrolizumab, with median DOR not reached at 26.9 months' follow-up and median PFS of 19.8 months. Lifileucel is already the first FDA-approved cell therapy for a solid tumor, on a 31.4% response rate in post-progression patients; first-line positioning would roughly double that benchmark and materially expand eligible patient volume for TIL production. TIL is among the most logistically demanding cell products, and the current result also underlines that TIL access is concentrated at specialist centers. Phase 3 TILVANCE-301 is the decisive test; the study was Iovance-funded.

🌐 Open WebOctober 24, 2026
McKesson report: only ~2 in 10 CAR-T-eligible patients receive treatment, and out-of-pocket travel costs are a named barrier

InspiroGene by McKesson's 2026 CGT Report finds that only about two in ten CAR-T-eligible patients actually receive treatment, with patient out-of-pocket costs (notably travel and lodging) and coverage delays among the most cited reasons referred patients never get treated. For a CDMO this is the most under-discussed bottleneck in the sector: the constraint is not manufacturing capacity but site geography, reimbursement friction and travel burden. It also means clients' commercial forecasts are systematically optimistic about how much capacity will actually be consumed. Rheumatologists report being only "minimally" to "moderately" prepared for the 77 late-stage autoimmune therapies, and 64% expect CGT care to decentralize into their own specialty while 65% of oncologists expect it to stay centralized at treatment centers — a split that directly determines where future cell therapy capacity gets built.
