🔬 Endpoints News🌐 Open WebOctober 24, 2026
The zola-cel death was not ICANS-related — and the mechanism is still unexplained

At MSToronto2026, Dr. Jeffrey Cohen gave the day-by-day timeline: motor symptoms day 17, high-dose cyclophosphamide re-administered day 41 with no alternative cause identified, septic pneumonia day 43, death day 44. The patient's CAR-T expansion was in line with other participants, the death was not ICANS-related, and the pathogenesis is "not fully understood." It occurred after the June 2026 data cutoff. What this means for RoslinCT: an unexplained death in a program with 190 patients treated, plus a rescue intervention (high-dose cyclophosphamide) that preceded the fatal event, means sponsors investigating this will scrutinize cell characterization, release testing, potency assays, and post-infusion product traceability far more than they did at the start. That scrutiny lands directly on the CDMO. It also means the safety narrative for autoimmune CAR-T is genuinely unsettled rather than closed, which pushes sponsor timelines out and makes comparability, chain-of-identity, and deviation-management capability a differentiator. Note also that BMS has not framed this as an ICANS event, which is a change from the initial company framing and should be reflected in any client conversation about autoimmune CAR-T risk.

🌐 MedPage Today · 🔬 Endpoints News (subscriber access)
🌐 Open WebOctober 24, 2026
Quell's CHILL nTreg trial at Oxford — a different autoimmune modality with a different manufacturing profile

The CHILL trial (NCT07473154) dosed its first patient at Oxford, the second site globally to deliver an engineered regulatory T cell therapy for autoimmune disease. The product, QEL-005, is autologous, lentivirally transduced, and engineered with a CD19 B-cell activated receptor; Phase 1/2 in diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis and difficult-to-treat RA, ~16 estimated participants, eight European sites, completion estimated August 2028. Why RoslinCT should care: Chief Investigator Christopher Buckley's framing — "CAR-T cells kill; they do not regulate" — is a direct strategic response to the deaths now accumulating in autoimmune CAR-T. If Treg approaches deliver comparable disease control, the autoimmune commercial thesis stops depending on CD19-directed depletion, and the manufacturing profile shifts: regulatory T cells need different expansion, characterization, release and potency work than cytotoxic CAR-T, though they run on the same autologous infrastructure. RoslinCT's ability to support multiple autologous cell modalities from one platform, and to articulate the CMC differences between them, is a stronger BD position than claiming generic cell therapy capability. Trial delivery required new patient pathways and cross-departmental collaboration, which is exactly the kind of operational support that differentiates a CDMO in this space.

🌐 Oxford Mail · ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07473154
🌐 Peer-reviewed clinical studyOctober 24, 2026
CAR-T hematotoxicity is becoming predictable — and it is now the dominant safety concern, not CRS or ICANS

A 96-patient Beijing Tongren Hospital cohort in BMC Cancer shows grade 3–4 neutropenia peaking at day 4 in 71.9% of CAR-T patients, with 34.7% still severely neutropenic at day 28, and a pre-infusion CAR-HEMATOTOX score that predicts the worst cases (aOR 4.04 for severe early neutropenia; aOR 8.22 and 4.20 for early and late thrombocytopenia). Bridging therapy predicted late neutropenia (aOR 4.25). Why RoslinCT should care: with CRS grade ≥3 in only 2.1% and ICANS in 10.4% at experienced centers, prolonged cytopenia is the safety issue that now dominates post-infusion care — and it is a serviceable, product-adjacent problem. It creates demand for tighter bridging-to-infusion scheduling, more responsive post-infusion logistics, and potentially a role supporting release testing and comparability around heavily pre-treated, low-count patients. It also reinforces the "make difficult therapies executable" position: the hard cases are now well-defined enough to design manufacturing and logistics plans around, and a CDMO that can name them is more credible than one that only cites throughput.

🌐 Bioengineer summary · 🌐 BMC Cancer original
🌐 Open WebOctober 24, 2026
APAC CAR T will be built around a small number of academic centers per country

The Asian Lymphoma Study Group's updated mantle cell lymphoma consensus, from 16 authors across 12 countries, states plainly that CAR T-cell therapy remains concentrated in major academic centers across the region, so eligibility often depends as much on geography as on disease biology, and scales all treatment intensity to local access. Why RoslinCT should care: this is an independent, non-US confirmation of the access constraint the McKesson report identifies domestically, and it says something concrete about how APAC demand will actually be distributed. Demand will cluster around a handful of centers per country with high volumes and complex logistics, not spread across community sites. That favors regional manufacturing and centralized distribution over broad site-of-care expansion, and it means the APAC opportunity is about a few high-volume, high-complexity relationships rather than a long tail of small accounts — a different BD motion from the US market, and one RoslinCT's existing Australia and Korea positioning can serve.

🌐 AJMC report · 🌐 Cancer Res Treat original
🌐 Trial registry (authoritative)October 25, 2026
BMS's first in vivo CAR-T autoimmune trial — and a direct challenge to the autologous manufacturing premise

ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07873931 documents BMS-986553, officially CD19 In Vivo CAR T Cells, in a Phase 1 study across six refractory autoimmune diseases (~60 patients, estimated start Oct 30 2026, primary completion 2032). Note on the superlative: aggregator coverage calling this the "world's first" is false, and "first big pharma" needs qualification — AbbVie already has an in vivo CAR-T autoimmune trial running through its $2.1B acquisition of Capstan (NCT06917742, CPTX2309 in RA and SLE, recruiting since April 2025). Why RoslinCT should care, and it is not comfortable: in vivo CAR-T converts a bespoke manufactured cell product into a vialed biologic administered in a patient's own body — no apheresis, no centralized manufacturing slot, no cryogenic chain, and potentially no lymphodepleting chemotherapy. If it delivers comparable B-cell depletion, it removes most of the operational value proposition that a cell therapy CDMO sells, and it does so in the autoimmune segment where BMS's own autologous program just produced a death with unexplained mechanism. There is a second-order effect that matters even more: the same logic is now being validated at low cost. Nepal has delivered its first locally manufactured CAR-T, with Dr. Luca Gattinoni (LIT- Leibniz Institute for Immunotherapy) explicitly framing it as "a model for other low- and middle-income countries." If local low-cost production can serve those markets, the addressable market does not route through Western CDMOs, and RoslinCT's APAC strategy should be built around partnerships and technology transfer rather than export manufacturing. The honest read is that CDMO value migrates toward harder-to-replicate work — in vivo and point-of-care platforms, complex allogeneic and Treg modalities, comparability and release-testing for a product that is never pre-infusion characterized — and away from autologous slot utilization. RoslinCT's BD message should be built for that shift rather than against it.

🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07873931 · 🌐 Oncodaily — Nepal's first locally manufactured CAR-T · 🌐 OncoDaily in vivo CAR-T review
🌐 Company press releaseOctober 23, 2026
Charles River, Labcorp and FDA collaboration — the FDA is building evidence standards with commercial labs

Charles River, Labcorp and the FDA signed a Research Collaboration Agreement to develop standardized, validated preclinical methods for quantifying and ranking drug abuse potential, which may inform regulatory assessment under the Controlled Substances Act. Not a CGT item, and it should not be presented as one. Why it belongs in RoslinCT's radar anyway: it shows the direction of the FDA's regulatory infrastructure agenda — co-designing validated evidence methods with accredited third-party laboratories rather than building capacity in-house. RoslinCT already sells process development and quality services into a regulatory environment that increasingly specifies what evidence it wants, and a client that can cite a partner-defined, regulator-co-designed method has a stronger pitch than one that offers testing alone. It also reinforces the cycle's consistent theme: the binding constraint across this sector is increasingly the generation and validation of evidence, not the manufacture of the product.

🌐 BioSpace / Business Wire
🌐 Company press releaseOctober 23, 2026
TG Therapeutics' azer-cel: the first allogeneic CAR-T in progressive MS, and the clearest signal yet that off-the-shelf cell therapy can reach into the CNS

azer-cel (azercabtagene zapreleucel), an allogeneic CD19 CAR-T licensed from Precision BioSciences, presented in a late-breaking oral at MSToronto2026. TG states this is the first reported clinical evaluation of an allogeneic CD19-directed CAR-T in progressive MS. Phase 1 TG-Azercel-101, 26 participants, six dose levels from 30M to 500M cells, bendamustine lymphodepletion, data cutoff Sept 3 2026, median follow-up 4.9 months: no dose-limiting toxicities, no Grade ≥3 CRS, no Grade ≥2 ICANS, no graft-versus-host disease, no immune effector cell-associated hemophagocytic syndrome, no Grade ≥3 infections. All 26 had progression independent of relapse activity in the prior 12 months despite high-efficacy therapy including prior anti-CD20; median baseline EDSS 6.0. EDSS stable or improved in 89% of evaluable participants at Day 90 (16/18) and 88% at Day 180 (7/8); three improved by at least 2.0 EDSS points at Day 90, one by 4.0. At Day 90, stable or improved walking 94% (16/17), upper-extremity function 94% (17/18), cognitive processing speed 78% (14/18); no gadolinium-enhancing T1 lesions and no new or enlarging T2 lesions in any of 22 MRI-evaluable participants. Peripheral blood B cells below 10 cells/µL in 96% (25/26); CSF from 21 participants showed a median 52.3% reduction in CD19-positive B-cell events at Day 10, with reconstitution from Day 42 favoring transitional and naïve populations. Why RoslinCT should read this closely, and there is real good news in it. First, the commercial model is the one that scales. azer-cel is allogeneic and off-the-shelf: no apheresis, no patient-specific manufacturing slot, no individualized chain of identity. For a CDMO, a universal donor cell product is a fundamentally more predictable business than one bespoke product per patient — and in MS the addressable population is measured in the tens of thousands, not the hundreds, which is the volume tier where autologous CAR-T economics have never worked. Second, the CNS signal is the strategically important part: CSF B-cell depletion of this depth is not achievable with approved anti-CD20 antibodies, and it is the reason people with progressive MS have no options after B-cell therapy fails. Demonstrating durable depletion in the CSF without a severe cytokine or neurotoxicity signal is precisely the profile that would support a neuro-immunology franchise. Third, a caution worth stating plainly: 26 patients, median 4.9 months, uncontrolled open-label design, and EDSS is a clinician-rated scale that is not immune to expectation bias when there is no comparator. "Stable or improved" in 16 of 18 at Day 90 is encouraging, not conclusive, and a 24-month read is where this claim will actually be tested. The competitive pressure this creates is on the autologous Treg and allogeneic approaches in the same space, including CRISPR Therapeutics' CTX112, which the registry shows opening a Phase 1/2 in refractory neurologic autoimmune disease including progressive MS, NMOSD, MOGAD, autoimmune encephalitis and stiff-person syndrome (NCT07804004, 220 participants, start Nov 1, 2026).

🌐 BioSpace / Business Wire · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06680037 · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07804004 (CRISPR CTX112)
🌐 Trial registry (authoritative)October 25, 2026
Correction: AbbVie's Capstan already has an in vivo CAR-T autoimmune trial dosing — the competitive set is larger than the headlines suggest

Verifying the BMS-986553 "world's first" claim this cycle surfaced a fact that narrows the "first big pharma" framing: AbbVie holds a Phase 1 first-in-human study of CPTX2309, Capstan's anti-CD8-targeted LNP carrying CD19 CAR mRNA with hepatic de-targeting, in healthy volunteers and in moderate-to-severe RA and SLE — ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06917742, recruiting since April 2025, 64 estimated participants, primary completion November 2027. AbbVie acquired Capstan in June 2025 for up to $2.1B, and the registry still lists Capstan as lead sponsor, but the asset now sits inside a large-cap pharma. AbbVie also holds an option relationship with Umoja's lentiviral programs, and Eli Lilly acquired both Orna (circular RNA) and Kelonia (integrating lentiviral) — buying both in vivo architectures deliberately rather than betting on one. Accurate ranking for in vivo CAR-T in autoimmune disease: the first programs overall are Chinese academic centers from mid-2025 (OL-CD19-GDT at Beijing GoBroad; LNP-mRNA and lentiviral studies at West China, Xinqiao, Chinese PLA General Hospital, Peking University People's Hospital); Capstan's CPTX2309 was the first from a Western company under a top-20 pharma owner and has been dosing autoimmune patients since 2025; BMS-986553 is the first large-cap pharma to run a dedicated in vivo autoimmune Phase 1 as a sponsored named commercial product. "World's first" is false and "first big pharma" needs qualification. Why this matters to RoslinCT specifically: the autoimmune in vivo field now has seventeen registered in vivo CAR-T studies, still mostly Chinese academic, but with Western participation arriving by two routes rather than one — AbbVie's stake in Capstan is an acquisition, while Myeloid's CRT-402 (NCT07778446) and the large-cap programmes running alongside it are internally developed. That tells you something about where the technology actually is and where the capital is not — and it also means the manufacturing question is being answered in multiple places at once. In vivo CAR-T removes the CDMO's most distinctive deliverable (the manufactured cell product) and replaces it with the vector or nanoparticle, which is a conventional biologic CDMO can make at scale. That is the honest strategic threat, and it argues that the durable CDMO position in autoimmune is not autologous cell manufacturing but the development and release-testing work that sits in front of it.

🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06917742 (Capstan CPTX2309)
🌐 Program + company press releasesOctober 25, 2026
ARPA-H is funding the end of the cold chain — and for a CDMO that is a bigger deal than it looks

ARPA-H has awarded up to $36.5M to CYBORGEL, a four-year UC Davis-led project testing whether therapeutic cells protected by intracellular polymer gels survive room-temperature storage, with a goal of more than three months of usable shelf life. Led by Cheemeng Tan with Mayo Clinic, the University of Georgia and Case Western Reserve, it builds on earlier work forming water-rich polymer networks inside living cells that halted cell division while maintaining cellular health. Two other teams are funded in parallel: CaseBioscience (up to $7.3M) and Draper ($7.3M), both under ARPA-H's BoSS (BioStabilization Systems) program, whose framing is unusually blunt about why this matters — biologics "must be kept frozen at ultra-cold temperatures in an unbroken cold chain from factory to patient," a dose worth hundreds of thousands of dollars "can be ruined from just one power outage, one shipping delay, or one freezer failure," and the cold chain "adds tens of billions of dollars in cost every year." Why RoslinCT should read this as strategically significant rather than as a curiosity. The cold chain is the structural reason the autologous cell therapy business has been so hard to scale, and it is the reason CDMOs have been able to sell value-added services layered on top of it. If cell therapies can travel at room temperature, three things change. First, site economics invert: the binding constraint on a cell therapy center today is liquid-nitrogen infrastructure rather than clinical capability, so removing it opens community and rural sites that a geographically distributed CDMO strategy has never needed to contemplate. Second, the distribution model changes — fewer centralized infusion centers means fewer shipment lanes and looser batch-slot planning, which is a direct subtraction from the slot utilization that autologous CDMO revenue rests on. Third, and most consequential, ambient stability moves value from the cell to the vector: a room-temperature-stable product is something manufactured in campaign mode rather than per patient, which is a fundamentally different — and lower-margin, lower-barrier — business than one bespoke product per patient. The honest counterpoint is that none of this is demonstrated yet: this is research funding, the award does not establish that three-month shelf life has been achieved, and Tan's stated benefits are prospective. But the direction of travel is a government priority, and RoslinCT's commercial narrative to clients should assume the cold chain becomes less defensible as a moat over the next three years rather than treating it as permanent infrastructure. The durable CDMO position in this world is analytical: stability formulation, comparability and shelf-life characterization, and the release-testing work that a stabilized product still requires — none of which is the same capability as autologous cell manufacturing, but all of which is defensible.

🌐 ARPA-H BoSS (BioStabilization Systems) program · 🌐 Bioengineer — CYBORGEL award · 🌐 BioSpace — CaseBioscience ambient preservation
🌐 Conference presentation + trial registryOctober 20, 2026
Duchenne’s competitive question has moved from the vector to the evidence package

Regenxbio's RGX-202 met its Phase 3 primary endpoint in Duchenne, with two drug-related serious adverse events that resolved — myocarditis in an 8-year-old and liver injury in a 10-year-old — and a single-arm design whose functional findings rest on nine boys at one year compared against weighted external groups rather than a placebo arm. Read as a service signal, this is the most instructive development on the site right now: the competitive question in Duchenne is no longer vector design or dose, it is whether a single-arm, surrogate-endpoint package will be accepted. Meanwhile Sarepta's Elevidys — the only approved Duchenne gene therapy — is under commercial pressure, with first-quarter 2026 gene therapy revenue of $331 million and Roche running a new study after a negative European opinion. For clients, the actionable point is that characterisation and comparability work — assays, external control matching, endpoint validation — now carries more regulatory value than a production tweak.

🌐 NeurologyLive — RGX-202 Phase 3 primary endpoint · 🌐 Medical Daily — conference data, safety detail and filing status · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05693142 (AFFINITY DUCHENNE) · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — RGX-202 approval filing intent · 🌐 ClinicalTrials.gov NCT07160634 (SGT-003) · 🌐 FDA — approved cellular and gene therapy products · 🌐 NeurologyLive — Elevidys vs external controls at 2 years · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — Elevidys revenue decline · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — Sarepta CEO change · 🌐 BioPharma Dive — Roche new Elevidys study after European setback
🏛️ Federal Register noticeOctober 16, 2026
The FDA’s rare disease framework will decide which evidence packages get approved

The FDA's “Fit-for-Purpose Rare Disease Framework Initiative” public meeting on October 28, 2026 (comments due December 11) will set the rules for surrogate endpoints, external control groups and single-arm designs — the exact three grounds on which RGX-121 was rejected in February 2026, and the same questions now facing RGX-202 in Duchenne. For a CDMO this is a business-model signal, not a compliance footnote: as rare-disease approval becomes more explicitly dependent on evidence packages, the work that clients will need to buy moves upstream from manufacturing into assay development, control-arm matching, endpoint validation and longitudinal data infrastructure. That is where a partner with a cell-therapy analytics and comparability capability can differentiate itself against a pure-play manufacturing vendor.

🌐 Federal Register 2026-21142 — rare disease framework notice
